### Lesson 27: Scarcity Error

#### Examples

##### Example 1: Experiment

The experiment was repeated and always showed the same result. ‘Only while stocks last,’ the adverts alert. ‘Today only,’ warn the posters. Gallery owners take advantage of the scarcity error by placing red ‘sold’ dots under most of their paintings, transforming the remaining works into rare items that must be snatched up quickly. We collect stamps, coins, vintage cars even when they serve no practical purpose. The post ofﬁce doesn’t accept the old stamps, the banks don’t take old coins, and the vintage cars are no longer allowed on the road. These are all side issues; the attraction is that they are in short supply. In one study, students were asked to arrange ten posters in order of attractiveness – with the agreement that afterward they keep one poster as a reward for their participation. Five minutes later, they were told that the poster with the third highest rating was no longer available. Then they were asked to judge all ten from scratch.

#### Steps

#### Step 1: Pause When Scarcity Signals

In conclusion: the typical response to scarcity is a lapse in clear thinking.

#### Best Practices

- ✅ When an item is framed as scarce or limited, pause and evaluate whether it actually meets your needs before rushing to buy.
- ✅ Remember that scarcity signals are often artificial: gallery owners place red 'sold' dots to make remaining paintings seem rare.
- ❌ We collect stamps, coins, and vintage cars even when they serve no practical purpose — the attraction is purely that they are in short supply.

#### Keep In Mind

You will encounter scarcity error whenever 'only while stocks last' or 'today only' signs make you feel compelled to buy something immediately.

#### Security & Safety Notes

- This bias mainly influences consumer and investment choices, leading people to make impulsive decisions under perceived time pressure instead of carefully assessing actual value.

#### Common Pitfalls

- These are all side issues; the attraction is that they are in short supply.

### Lesson 28: Base-Rate Neglect

#### Examples

##### Example 1: Illustrative Example

Here is a second example: a young man is stabbed and fatally injured. Which of these is more likely? A) The attacker is a Russian immigrant and imports combat knives illegally, or B) the attacker is a middle-class American. You know the drill now: option B is much more likely because there are a million times more middle-class Americans than there are Russian knife importers. In medicine, base-rate neglect plays an important role. For example, migraines can point (among others) to a viral infection or a brain tumour. However, viral infections are much more common (in other words, they have a higher base rate), so doctors assess patients for these ﬁrst before testing for tumours. This is very reasonable.

#### Steps



#### Step 1: Check Base Rates First

Before judging the likelihood of anything, identify the actual statistical distribution. Germany has 10,000 times more truck drivers than Frankfurt has literature professors — so a Mozart-loving, glasses-wearing German is far more likely to be a truck driver. Always ask: what is the base rate for this category?

#### Step 2: Test the Most Likely Cause First

Whether in medicine or business, investigate the most common explanation before chasing exotic ones. As medical students are taught: 'When you hear hoofbeats behind you, don't expect to see a zebra.' Viral infections are far more common than brain tumours, so test for those first.

#### Step 3: Guard Against Base-Rate Neglect

Before investing in a venture or admiring a success story, remember that only the survivors are visible. According to data from the U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics, approximately 20% of new businesses fail within their first five years, so the chance of any one becoming the next Google is almost zero. Look at the full distribution, not just the winners.

#### Best Practices

- ✅ Before diagnosing a rare condition, always compare its base rate against more common explanations with similar symptoms.
- ✅ In medical school, residents spend a lot of time purging base-rate neglect: test for the most likely cause first, not the most interesting one.
- ❌ A Russian knife importer is much less likely to stab you than a middle-class American, because there are a million times more middle-class Americans.

#### Keep In Mind

You will encounter base-rate neglect whenever a vivid but rare cause seems more plausible than a common but boring one, such as fearing shark attacks while ignoring heart disease.

#### Security & Safety Notes

- In medical school, residents spend a lot of time purging base-rate neglect.
- If, like me, you are not a wine connoisseur, the only lifeline you have is the base rate.

#### Common Pitfalls

- Scientists call this fallacy base-rate neglect: a disregard of fundamental distribution levels.
- See also Survivorship Bias (ch. 1); Neglect of Probability (ch. 26); Gambler’s Fallacy (ch. 29); Conjunction Fallacy (ch. 41); The Problem with Averages (ch. 55); Information Bias (ch. 59); Ambiguity Aversion (ch. 80)

### Lesson 29: Gambler’s Fallacy

#### Examples

##### Example 1: Experiment

The Monte Carlo example and the IQ experiment show that people believe in the ‘balancing force of the universe’. This is the gambler’s fallacy. However, with independent events, there is no harmonising force at work: a ball cannot remember each spin is independent. Despite this, one of my friends enters the weekly Mega Millions numbers into a spreadsheet, and then plays those that have appeared the least. All this work is for naught. He is another victim of the gambler’s fallacy. The following joke illustrates this phenomenon: a mathematician is afraid of ﬂying due to the small risk of a terrorist attack. So, on every ﬂight he takes a bomb with him in his hand luggage. ‘The probability of having a bomb on the plane is very low,’ he reasons, ‘and the probability of having two bombs on the same plane is virtually zero!’ A coin is ﬂipped three times and lands on heads on each occasion.

##### Example 2: Illustrative Example

An example: if you are experiencing record cold where you live, it is likely that the temperature will return to normal values over the next few days. If the weather functioned like a casino, there would be a 50% chance that the temperature would rise and a 50% chance that it would drop. But the weather is not like a casino. Complex feedback mechanisms in the atmosphere ensure that extremes balance themselves out. In other cases, however, extremes intensify. For example, the rich tend to get richer. A stock that shoots up creates its own demand to a certain extent, simply because it stands out so much – a sort of reverse compensation effect. So, take a closer look at the independent and interdependent events around you.

#### Steps

#### Step 1: Examine the Details

So, take a closer look at the independent and interdependent events around you.

#### Best Practices

- ✅ Take a closer look at the independent and interdependent events around you: with independent events, there is no harmonising force at work.
- ✅ A ball cannot remember each spin is independent; each spin is truly independent.
- ❌ One of my friends enters the weekly Mega Millions numbers into a spreadsheet and plays those that have appeared the least — all this work is for naught.

#### Keep In Mind

You will encounter the gambler's fallacy whenever you believe a coin is 'due' for heads after a streak of tails, or when you think a stock that has fallen must soon rise.

#### Security & Safety Notes

- However, with independent events, there is no harmonising force at work: a ball cannot remember each spin is independent.
- The following joke illustrates this phenomenon: a mathematician is afraid of ﬂying due to the small risk of a terrorist attack.

#### Common Pitfalls

- This is the gambler’s fallacy.
- The friend is another victim of the gambler’s fallacy.
- The following joke illustrates this phenomenon: a mathematician is afraid of ﬂying due to the small risk of a terrorist attack.

### Lesson 30: The Anchor

#### Examples

##### Example 1: Experiment

The psychologist Amos Tversky conducted an experiment involving a wheel of fortune. He had participants spin it, and afterward, they were asked for an estimate of United Nations member states. Their guesses conﬁrmed the anchor effect: the highest estimates came from people who had spun high numbers on the wheel. Researchers Russo and Shoemaker asked students in what year Attila the Hun suffered his crushing defeat in Europe. Just like the example with social security numbers, the participants were anchored – this time with the last digits of their telephone number. The result? People with higher numbers chose later years and vice versa. (If you were wondering, Attila’s demise came about in 453.) Another experiment: students and professional real-estate agents were given a tour of a house and asked to estimate its value. Beforehand, they were informed about a (randomly generated) listed sales price.

##### Example 2: The Five-Million-Dollar Anchor (The Anchor)

My boss was a pro when it came to using anchors. In his ﬁrst conversation with any client, he made sure to ﬁx an opening price, which, by the way, almost criminally exceeded our internal costs: ‘I’ll tell you this now so you’re not surprised when you receive the quote, Mr. So-and-So: we’ve just completed a similar project for one of your competitors and it was in the range of ﬁve million dollars.’ The anchor was dropped: the price negotiations started at exactly five million. See also Framing (ch. 42)

#### Steps

#### Step 1: Document Your Thinking

He asked his students to write down the last two digits of their social security numbers and then decide if they would be willing to spend that amount on the wine.

#### Best Practices

- ✅ Before negotiating, research the independent market value independently rather than letting the other party set the opening price.
- ✅ Be aware that random numbers — like the last digits of a social security number or a listing price — can anchor your estimates.
- ❌ A share price is never 'low' or 'high' because of its peak price; it is only worth buying if its future prospects justify the current price.

#### Keep In Mind

You will encounter the anchor effect whenever a real-estate agent suggests a listing price before you form your own estimate, or when a boss drops a five-million-dollar figure before negotiations even begin.

#### Security & Safety Notes

- The professor asked his students to write down the last two digits of their social security numbers and then decide if they would be willing to spend that amount on the wine.
- The social security digits worked as an anchor – albeit in a hidden and misleading way.

#### Common Pitfalls

- Researchers Russo and Shoemaker asked students in what year Attila the Hun suffered his crushing defeat in Europe.

### Lesson 31: Induction

#### Examples

##### Example 1: The Christmas Goose (Induction)

A farmer feeds a goose. At ﬁrst, the shy animal is hesitant, wondering ‘What’s going on here? Why is he feeding me?’ This continues for weeks until, eventually, the goose’s scepticism gives way. After months, the goose is sure that ‘The farmer has my best interests at heart.’ Each additional day’s feeding conﬁrms this. Fully convinced of the man’s benevolence, the goose is amazed when he takes it out of its enclosure on Christmas Day – and slaughters it. The Christmas goose fell victim to inductive thinking, the inclination to draw universal certainties from individual observations. Philosopher David Hume used this allegory back in the eighteenth century to warn of its pitfalls. However, it’s not just geese that are susceptible to it.

##### Example 2: The Stock Market Prophet (Induction)

As the stock continues to rise, even after months, his apprehension turns into excitement: ‘This stock may never come down’ – especially since every day this is the case. After half a year, he invests his life savings in it, turning a blind eye to the huge cluster risk this poses. Later, the man will pay for his foolish investment. He has fallen hook, line and sinker for induction. Inductive thinking doesn’t have to be a road to ruin, though. In fact, you can exploit it to make a fortune by sending emails. Here’s how: put together two stock market forecasts – one predicting that prices will rise next month and one warning of a drop. Send the ﬁrst email to 50,000 people, and the second email to a different set of 50,000.

##### Example 3: Hypothetical Scenario

We imagine that we will not be randomly beaten up on the street. We expect that our hearts will still be beating tomorrow. These are conﬁdences without which we cannot live, but we must remember that certainties are always provisional. As Benjamin Franklin said, ‘Nothing is certain but death and taxes.’ Induction seduces us and leads us to conclusions such as: ‘Mankind has always survived, so we will be able to tackle any future challenges, too.’ Sounds good in theory, but what we fail to realise is that such a statement can only come from a species that has lasted until now. To assume that our existence to date is an indication of our future survival is a serious ﬂaw in reasoning. Probably the most serious of all. See also False Causality (ch.37); Survivorship Bias (ch. 1)

#### Steps

#### Step 1: Remember the Key Point

These are conﬁdences without which we cannot live, but we must remember that certainties are always provisional.

#### Step 2: Question Past Patterns

As Benjamin Franklin said, ‘Nothing is certain but death and taxes.’ Induction seduces us and leads us to conclusions such as: ‘Mankind has always survived, so we will be able to tackle any future challenges, too.’ Sounds good in theory, but what we fail to realise is that such a statement can only come from a species that has lasted until now.

#### Best Practices

- ✅ These are conﬁdences without which we cannot live, but we must remember that certainties are always provisional.
- ✅ As Benjamin Franklin said, ‘Nothing is certain but death and taxes.’ Induction seduces us and leads us to conclusions such as: ‘Mankind has always survived, so we will be able to tackle any future challenges, too.’ Sounds good in theory, but what we fail to realise is that such a statement can only come from a species that has lasted until now.

#### Keep In Mind

- These are conﬁdences without which we cannot live, but we must remember that certainties are always provisional.
- As Benjamin Franklin said, ‘Nothing is certain but death and taxes.’ Induction seduces us and leads us to conclusions such as: ‘Mankind has always survived, so we will be able to tackle any future challenges, too.’ Sounds good in theory, but what we fail to realise is that such a statement can only come from a species that has lasted until now.

#### Security & Safety Notes

- After half a year, the investor invests his life savings in it, turning a blind eye to the huge cluster risk this poses.
- Here’s how: put together two stock market forecasts – one predicting that prices will rise next month and one warning of a drop.

#### Common Pitfalls

- Philosopher David Hume used this allegory back in the eighteenth century to warn of its pitfalls.
- After half a year, the investor invests his life savings in it, turning a blind eye to the huge cluster risk this poses.
- Inductive thinking doesn’t have to be a road to ruin, though.

### Lesson 32: Loss Aversion

#### Examples

##### Example 1: The Hedonic Treadmill (Loss Aversion)

On a scale of 1 to 10, how good do you feel today? Now consider what would bring you up to a perfect 10. That vacation in the Caribbean you’ve always dreamed of? A promotion or career advancement? Next question: what would make you drop down by the same number of points? Paralysis, Alzheimer’s, cancer, depression, war, hunger, torture, ﬁnancial ruin, damage to your reputation, losing your best friend, your children getting kidnapped, blindness, death? The long list of possibilities makes us realise the negative outcomes; in short, there are more bad things than good – and they are far more consequential. In our evolutionary past, this was even more the case.

#### Steps

#### Step 1: Recognize the Memory Bias

We remember bad behaviour longer than good — except, of course, when it comes to our own actions.

#### Best Practices

- ✅ We remember bad behaviour longer than good — except, of course, when it comes to ourselves.
- ✅ Before making a risky decision, evaluate the potential loss and the potential gain separately rather than focusing only on avoiding loss.
- ❌ From an employee's perspective, risk aversion is rational: the potential upside of a bonus is often outweighed by the downside of job loss.

#### Keep In Mind

- We remember bad behaviour longer than good – except, of course, when it comes to ourselves.

#### Security & Safety Notes

- Paralysis, Alzheimer’s, cancer, depression, war, hunger, torture, ﬁnancial ruin, damage to your reputation, losing your best friend, your children getting kidnapped, blindness, death?
- Everything leads to your rapid departure from the game of life – carelessness on the hunt, an inﬂamed tendon, exclusion from the group and so on.

#### Common Pitfalls

- Paralysis, Alzheimer’s, cancer, depression, war, hunger, torture, ﬁnancial ruin, damage to your reputation, losing your best friend, your children getting kidnapped, blindness, death?
- The reality is: employees tend to be risk-averse.
- From their perspective, this aversion makes perfect sense: why risk something that brings them, at best, a nice bonus, and at worst, a pink slip?

### Lesson 33: Social Loafing

#### Examples

##### Example 1: The Rope-Pulling Experiment (Social Loafing)

In 1913 Maximilian Ringelmann, a French engineer, studied the performance of horses. He concluded that the power of two animals pulling a coach did not equal twice the power of a single horse. Surprised by this result, he extended his research to humans. He had men pull a rope and measured the force applied by each individual. On average, if two people were pulling together, each invested just 93% of their individual strength, when three pulled together, it was 85%, and with eight people, just 49%. Science calls this the social loaﬁng effect. It occurs when individual performance is not directly visible; it blends in to the group effort. It occurs among rowers, but not in relay races, because here, individual contributions are evident.

#### Steps

#### Step 1: Measure Individual Contributions

In conclusion: people behave differently in groups than when alone (otherwise there would be no groups).

#### Best Practices

- ✅ In conclusion: people behave differently in groups than when alone (otherwise there would be no groups).
- ✅ Make individual contributions visible in team tasks to prevent social loafing.
- ❌ When two people pull together, each invests just 93% of their individual strength; with eight people, it drops to 49%.

#### Keep In Mind

You will encounter social loafing whenever individual performance blends into group effort and people reduce their effort because they feel less accountable.

#### Security & Safety Notes

- For the same reason, teams tend to take bigger risks than their members would take on their own.
- This phenomenon, known as risky shift, can lead groups to make more extreme decisions than individuals would alone — a concern in high-stakes contexts like corporate strategy or military planning.

#### Common Pitfalls

- So what stops us from putting our feet up completely and letting the others do all the hard work?
- For the same reason, teams tend to take bigger risks than their members would take on their own.
- This phenomenon, known as risky shift, can lead groups to make more extreme decisions than individuals would alone — a concern in high-stakes contexts like corporate strategy or military planning.

### Lesson 34: Exponential Growth

#### Examples

##### Example 1: Paper Folding and Exponential Growth

A piece of paper is folded in two, then in half again, again and again. How thick will it be after 50 folds? Write down your guess before you continue reading. Second task. Choose between these options: A) Over the next 30 days, I will give you $1,000 a day. B) Over the next 30 days, I will give you a cent on the ﬁrst day, two cents on the second day, four cents on the third day, eight cents on the fourth day, and so on. Don’t think too long about it: A or B? Are you ready?

##### Example 2: Hypothetical Scenario

You can look it up in the notes section.) Another example: ‘Inﬂation is at 5%.’ Whoever hears this thinks: ‘That’s not so bad, what’s 5% anyway?’ Let’s quickly calculate the doubling time: 70 divided by 5 = 14 years. In 14 years, a dollar will be worth only half what it is today – a catastrophe for anyone who has a savings account. Suppose you are a journalist and learn that the number of registered dogs in your city is rising by 10% a year. Which headline do you put on your article? Certainly not: ‘Dog registrations increasing by 10%.’ No one will care. Instead, announce: ‘Deluge of dogs: twice as many mutts in 7 years’ time!’ Nothing that grows exponentially grows for ever. Most politicians, economists and journalists forget that. Such growth will eventually reach a limit.

##### Example 3: The Persian Rice and Chessboard Legend

The ancient Persians were well aware that people struggled with percentage growth. Here is a local tale: there was once a wise courtier, who presented the king with a chessboard. Moved by the gift, the king said to him: ‘Tell me how I can thank you.’ The courtier replied: ‘Your Highness, I want nothing more than for you to cover the chessboard with rice, putting one grain of rice on the ﬁrst square, and then on every subsequent square, twice the previous number of grains.’ The king was astonished: ‘It is an honour to you, dear courtier, that you present such a modest request.’ But how much rice is that? The king guessed a sack. Only when his servants began the task – placing a grain on the ﬁrst square, two grains of rice on the second square, four grains of rice on the third, and so on – did he realise that he would need more rice than was growing on earth. When it comes to growth rates, do not trust your intuition. You don’t have any. What really helps is a calculator, or, with low growth rates, the magic number of 70.

#### Steps

#### Step 1: Question Your Intuition

When it comes to growth rates, do not trust your intuition.

#### Best Practices

- ❌ When it comes to growth rates, do not trust your intuition.

#### Keep In Mind

- Only when his servants began the task – placing a grain on the ﬁrst square, two grains of rice on the second square, four grains of rice on the third, and so on – did the king realise that he would need more rice than was growing on earth.

#### Security & Safety Notes

- Today, things are different. ‘Each year, the number of traffic accidents rises by 7%,’ warns a politician.
- So what the politician is saying is: ‘The number of trafﬁc accidents doubles every 10 years.’ Pretty alarming. (You may ask: ‘Why the number 70?’ This has to do with a mathematical concept called logarithm.

#### Common Pitfalls

- **Problem:** Falling prey to the bias without awareness
  **Solution:** Regularly review decisions and seek external feedback

### Lesson 35: Winner’s Curse

#### Examples

##### Example 1: The Texas Oil Auction (Winner's Curse)

Texas in the 1950s. A piece of land is being auctioned. Ten oil companies are vying for it. Each has made an estimate of how much the site is worth. The valuations range from tens of millions to over a hundred million dollars, reflecting the uncertainty about the site’s true worth. The higher the price climbs during the auction, the more ﬁrms exit the bidding. Finally, one company submits the highest bid and wins. Champagne corks pop.

##### Example 2: Hypothetical Scenario

Imagine that you and an opponent are invited to take part in such an auction. The rules: whoever makes the highest offer gets the $100 bill, and – most importantly – when this happens, both bidders have to pay their ﬁnal offer. How high will you go? From your perspective, it makes sense to pay $20, $30 or $40. Your opponent does the same. Even $99 seems like a reasonable offer for a $100 bill. Now, your competitor offers $100. If this remains the highest bid, he will come away breaking even (paying $100 for $100), whereas you will simply have to cough up $99.

##### Example 3: The $100 Bill Auction Dilemma

Write this number on a piece of paper and don’t go a cent over it. See also Endowment Effect (ch. 23)

#### Steps

#### Step 1: Accept the Core Wisdom

In conclusion: accept this piece of wisdom about auctions from Warren Buffett: ‘Don’t go.’ If auctions are unavoidable in your field, establish a maximum bid and reduce it by a margin to account for the winner’s curse.

#### Best Practices

- ✅ Accept Warren Buffett's wisdom: 'Don't go' to auctions.
- ✅ If auctions are inevitable, establish a maximum bid and reduce it by a margin to account for the winner's curse.
- ❌ Astoundingly, more than half of all acquisitions destroy value, according to a McKinsey study.

#### Keep In Mind

You will encounter the winner's curse whenever the highest bidder in an auction turns out to be the economic loser, or when Walmart's tender process drives suppliers to ruinous prices.

#### Security & Safety Notes

- And if Walmart plans to introduce a new detergent and asks for tenders from ﬁve suppliers, that’s nothing more than an auction – with the risk of the winner’s curse.
- The auctioning of everyday life has now reached tradesmen, too, thanks to the Internet.

#### Common Pitfalls

- And if Walmart plans to introduce a new detergent and asks for tenders from ﬁve suppliers, that’s nothing more than an auction – with the risk of the winner’s curse.
- Astoundingly, more than half of all acquisitions destroy value, according to a McKinsey study.

---

### Lesson 36: Fundamental Attribution Error

#### Examples

##### Example 1: Experiment

Opening the newspaper, you learn that another CEO has been forced to step down because of bad results. In the sports section, you read that your team’s winning season was thanks to player X or coach Y. In history books, you learn that the success of the French army in the early 1800s is a testament to Napoleon’s superb leadership and strategy. ‘Every story has a face’, it seems. Indeed this is an ironclad rule in every newsroom. Always on the lookout for the ‘people angle’, journalists (and their readers) take this principle one step further, and thus fall prey to the fundamental attribution error. This describes the tendency to overestimate individuals’ inﬂuence and underestimate external, situational factors. In 1967, researchers at Duke University set up the following experiment: participants read an argument either lauding or vilifying Fidel Castro. They were informed that the author of the text had been allocated the viewpoint regardless of his true political views; he was just making a coherent argument.

##### Example 2: Anecdote

Pay close attention to the dance of influences to which the actors are subjected. See also Story Bias (ch. 13); Swimmer’s Body Illusion (ch. 2); Salience Effect (ch. 83); News Illusion (ch. 99); Halo Effect (ch. 38); Fallacy of the Single Cause (ch. 97)

#### Steps

#### Step 1: Separate Action from Character

In conclusion: as much as we are fascinated by the spectacle of life, the people on stage are not perfect, self-governed individuals.

#### Best Practices

- ✅ In conclusion: as much as we are fascinated by the spectacle of life, the people on stage are not perfect, self-governed individuals.
- ✅ Pay close attention to the dance of influences to which the actors are subjected before assigning blame or praise.
- ❌ Every story has a face — but that face often obscures the situational factors that truly drove the outcome.

#### Keep In Mind

You will encounter fundamental attribution error whenever you read that a CEO was forced out for bad results and attribute the failure solely to their decisions, ignoring market conditions, luck, and external shocks.

#### Security & Safety Notes

- Banishment meant certain death, and those who actively opted for the solitary life – of whom there were some – fared no better and also disappeared from the gene pool.
- In conclusion: as much as we are fascinated by the spectacle of life, the people on stage are not perfect, self-governed individuals.

#### Common Pitfalls

- Which sounds a little like ﬁnancial markets and climate issues, don’t you agree?
- See also Story Bias (ch. 13); Swimmer’s Body Illusion (ch. 2); Salience Effect (ch. 83); News Illusion (ch. 99); Halo Effect (ch. 38); Fallacy of the Single Cause (ch. 97)

### Lesson 37: False Causality

#### Examples

##### Example 1: Hebridean Head Lice and False Causality

For the inhabitants of the Hebrides, a chain of islands north of Scotland, head lice were a part of life. If the lice left their host, he became sick and feverish. Therefore, to dispel the fever, sick people had lice put in their hair intentionally. There was a method to their madness: as soon as the lice had settled in again, the patient improved. In one city, a study revealed that in each blaze, the more ﬁreﬁghters called out to ﬁght it the greater the ﬁre damage. The mayor imposed an immediate hiring freeze and cut the firefighting budget. Both stories come from German physics professors Hans-Peter Beck-Bornholdt and Hans-Hermann Dubben. In their book (unfortunately there is no English version), they illustrate the muddling of cause and effect.

##### Example 2: Corporate Boardrooms and False Causality

We may smirk at these stories, but false causality leads us astray practically every day. Consider the headline ‘Employee motivation leads to higher corporate proﬁts.’ Does it? People may simply be more motivated because the company is doing well. Another headline touts that the more women on a corporate board, the more proﬁtable the ﬁrm is. But is that really how it works? Or do highly proﬁtable ﬁrms simply tend to recruit more women to their boards? Business-book authors and consultants often operate with similar false – or at least fuzzy – causalities. In the 90s, there was no one holier than the then head of the Federal Reserve, Alan Greenspan.

##### Example 3: Real-World Story

A further example: scientists found that long periods in the hospital affected patients adversely. This was music to health insurers’ ears; they, of course, are keen to make stays as brief as possible. But, clearly, patients who are discharged immediately are healthier than those who must stay on for treatment. This hardly makes long stays detrimental. Or, take this headline: ‘Fact: Women who use shampoo XYZ every day have stronger hair.’ Though the context can be substantiated scientiﬁcally, this statement says very little – least of all that the shampoo makes your hair stronger. It is simply the other way round: women with strong hair tend to use shampoo XYZ – and possibly because the label says ‘especially for thick hair.’ Recently I read that students get better grades at school if their homes contain a lot of books. This study was surely a shot in the arm for booksellers, but it is another ﬁne example of false causality.

#### Steps

#### Step 1: Look for a Third Variable

In conclusion: correlation is not causality.

#### Step 2: Examine the Details

Take a closer look at linked events: sometimes what is presented as the cause turns out to be the effect, and vice versa.

#### Best Practices

- ✅ In conclusion: correlation is not causality.
- ✅ Take a closer look at linked events: sometimes what is presented as the cause turns out to be the effect, and vice versa.
- ❌ Both stories come from German physics professors Hans-Peter Beck-Bornholdt and Hans-Hermann Dubben, who illustrate the muddling of cause and effect.

#### Keep In Mind

You will encounter false causality whenever a headline claims 'Employee motivation leads to higher profits' without considering that profitable companies simply have more resources to motivate employees.

#### Security & Safety Notes

- For the inhabitants of the Hebrides, a chain of islands north of Scotland, head lice were a part of life.
- In one city, a study revealed that in each blaze, the more ﬁreﬁghters called out to ﬁght it the greater the ﬁre damage.

#### Common Pitfalls

- This hardly makes long stays detrimental.
- See also Coincidence (ch. 24); Association Bias (ch. 48); Clustering Illusion (ch. 3); Story Bias (ch. 13); Induction (ch. 31); Beginner’s Luck (ch. 49)

---

### Lesson 38: Halo Effect

#### Examples

In March 2000, Cisco was the world's most valuable company, with journalists praising its customer service, strategy, acquisitions, culture, and charismatic CEO. When the stock crashed 80% the following year, those same 'strengths' were suddenly reframed as weaknesses – though nothing about the company had actually changed. Edward Thorndike discovered nearly a century ago that a single quality like beauty or social status creates a disproportionate impression, which is why attractive people are automatically judged as more intelligent, why teachers give better-looking students higher grades, and why Roger Federer endorsing a coffee machine seems convincing.

#### Steps

#### Step 1: Question Transfer of First Impressions

What emerges is often uncomfortable, but it typically provides useful insights for improving future decisions.

#### Best Practices

- ✅ What emerges is often uncomfortable, but it typically provides useful insights for improving future decisions.

#### Keep In Mind

You will encounter the halo effect whenever a charismatic CEO's reputation makes you overlook operational weaknesses, or when a beautiful spokesperson makes you trust a product.

#### Security & Safety Notes

- What had changed, in the wake of the dot-com crash, was demand for Cisco’s product – and that was through no fault of the firm.
- All that needs to register is the attractive face, dream lifestyle – and that product.

#### Common Pitfalls

- The halo effect always works the same way: we take a simple-to-obtain or remarkable fact or detail, such as a company’s ﬁnancial situation, and extrapolate conclusions from there that are harder to nail down, such as the merit of its management or the feasibility of its strategy.
- See also Fundamental Attribution Error (ch. 36); Salience Effect (ch. 83); Swimmer’s Body Illusion (ch. 2); Contrast Effect (ch. 10); Expectations (ch. 62)

---

### Lesson 39: Alternative Paths

#### Examples

##### Example 1: Russian Roulette for $10 Million (Alternative Paths)

You arrange to meet with a Russian oligarch in a forest just outside your city. He arrives shortly after you, carrying a suitcase and a gun. Placing the suitcase on the hood of his car, he opens it so you can see it is ﬁlled to the brim with stacks of money – $10 million in total. ‘Want to play Russian roulette?’ he asks. ‘Pull the trigger once, and all this is yours.’ The revolver contains a single bullet; the other ﬁve chambers are empty. You consider your options. $10 million would change your life. You would never have to work again. You will ﬁnally move from collecting stamps to collecting sports cars! You accept the challenge. You put the revolver to your temple and squeeze the trigger.

##### Example 2: Hypothetical Scenario

Suppose that, after twenty years, your hard-working neighbour has saved up $10 million. A journalist comes along one day and puts together a piece on the more afﬂuent residents in the area – complete with photos of the magniﬁcent buildings and the beautiful second wives that you and your neighbour have accrued. He comments on the interior design and the exquisite landscaping. However, the crucial difference between the two of you remains hidden from view: the risk that lurks behind each of the $10 million. For this the journalist would need to recognise the alternative paths. But not only journalists are underachievers at this skill. We all are. Alternative paths are all the outcomes that happened, but did not.

##### Example 3: Real-World Story

To a rational mind, ten million dollars that comes about through a huge risk is worth less than the same sum earned by years of drudgery. (An accountant disagrees, though.) Recently, I was at a dinner with an American friend who suggested tossing a coin to decide who should pay the bill. The situation was uncomfortable for me, since he was my guest in Switzerland. ‘Next time I’ll pay, whether here or in New York,’ I promised. He thought for a moment and said, ‘Considering the alternative paths, you’ve actually already paid for half of this dinner.’ In conclusion: risk is not directly visible. Therefore, always consider what the alternative paths are. Success that comes about through risky dealings is, to a rational mind, of less worth than success achieved the ‘boring’ way (for example, with laborious work as a lawyer, a dentist, a ski instructor, a pilot, a hairdresser or a consultant). Yes, looking at alternative paths from the outside is a difﬁcult task. Looking at them from the inside is an almost impossible task. Your brain will do everything to convince you that your success is warranted – no matter how risky your dealings are – and will obscure any thought of paths other than the one you are on.

#### Steps

#### Step 1: Consider the Alternative Path

Therefore, always consider what the alternative paths are.

#### Best Practices

- ✅ Therefore, always consider what the alternative paths are.

#### Keep In Mind

You will encounter alternative-path neglect whenever you attribute your success solely to your skill while ignoring the risky path you took that would have resulted in total loss.

#### Security & Safety Notes

- You consider your options. $10 million would change your life.
- However, the crucial difference between the two of you remains hidden from view: the risk that lurks behind each of the $10 million.

#### Common Pitfalls

- Suppose that, after twenty years, your hard-working neighbour has saved up $10 million.
- However, the crucial difference between the two of you remains hidden from view: the risk that lurks behind each of the $10 million.
- But, unlike you, the neighbour risked no alternative path that would have cost him his fortune – or his life.

---

### Lesson 40: Forecast Illusion

#### Examples

##### Example 1: Real-World Story

‘Facebook to be number one entertainment platform in three years.’ ‘Regime shift in North Korea in two years.’ ‘Sour grapes for France as Argentinian wines expected to dominate.’ ‘Euro collapse likely.’ ‘Low-cost space flights by 2025.’ ‘No more crude oil in 15 years.’ Every day, experts bombard us with predictions, but how reliable are they? Until recently, no one bothered to check. Then along came Philip Tetlock. Over a period of ten years, he evaluated 28,361 predictions from 284 self-appointed professionals. The result: in terms of accuracy, the experts fared only marginally better than a random forecast generator. Ironically, the media darlings were among the poorest performers; and of those the worst were the prophets of doom and disintegration. Examples of their far-fetched forecasts included the collapse of Canada, Nigeria, China, India, Indonesia, South Africa, Belgium and the E.U. None of these countries has imploded. ‘There are two kinds of forecasters: those who don’t know, and those who don’t know they don’t know,’ wrote Harvard economist John Kenneth Galbraith.

#### Steps

#### Step 1: Stay Skeptical of Predictions

Whenever I hear one, I make sure to smile, no matter how bleak it is.

#### Step 2: Treat Expert Predictions with Skepticism

If he is an employee, he risks losing his job when his predictions fail.

#### Best Practices

- ✅ Whenever I hear a prediction, I make sure to smile, no matter how bleak it is.
- ✅ The media darlings were among the poorest performers; and of those the worst were the prophets of doom and disintegration.
- ❌ Studies suggest that experts who are completely off the mark often face no penalties — either in terms of financial compensation or in loss of reputation.

#### Keep In Mind

You will encounter the forecast illusion every time an expert bombards you with a prediction about Facebook, North Korea, or the euro collapse — none of which has proven reliably forecastable.

#### Security & Safety Notes

- ‘Facebook to be number one entertainment platform in three years.’ ‘Regime shift in North Korea in two years.’ ‘Sour grapes for France as Argentinian wines expected to dominate.’ ‘Euro collapse likely.’ ‘Low-cost space flights by 2025.’ ‘No more crude oil in 15 years.’ Every day, experts bombard us with predictions, but how reliable are they?
- If they are completely off the mark, they face no penalties – either in terms of ﬁnancial compensation or in loss of reputation.

#### Common Pitfalls

- The problem is that experts enjoy free rein with limited negative consequences.
- I never have, and I never will.’ See also Expectations (ch. 62); Planning Fallacy (ch. 91); Authority Bias (ch. 9); Hindsight Bias (ch. 14); Overconfidence Effect (ch. 15); Illusion of Control (ch. 17); Hedonic Treadmill (ch. 46); Black Swan (ch. 75)

---

### Lesson 41: Conjunction Fallacy

#### Examples

##### Example 1: Anecdote

A) Chris works for a bank or B) Chris works for a bank in New York, where his ofﬁce is on the twenty-fourth floor, overlooking Central Park.’ Here’s another example: What is more likely? A) ‘Seattle airport is closed. Flights are cancelled.’ B) ‘Seattle airport is closed due to bad weather. Flights are cancelled.’ A or B? This time, you have it: A is more likely since B implies that an additional condition has been met, namely bad weather. A bomb threat, accident or strike closed the airport; however, when faced with a ‘plausible’ story, we don’t stop to consider such things. Now that you are aware of this, try it out with friends. You will see that most pick B.

#### Steps

#### Step 1: Compare Graduates to Non-Graduates

In conclusion: forget about left brains and right brains.

#### Step 2: Remember the Key Point

With important decisions, remember that, at the intuitive level, we have a soft spot for plausible stories.

#### Step 3: Watch for Plausible-Sounding Stories

When you encounter a convincing narrative, remember that adding specific details makes a story seem more plausible but actually reduces its probability. The conjunction fallacy strikes when a subset seems larger than the entire set — which by definition cannot be the case. Experts are not immune: at an international conference, academics felt much more strongly about a forecast with a dramatic causal story than an identical forecast without it. With important decisions, remember that at the intuitive level we have a soft spot for plausible stories — be on the lookout for convenient details and happy endings.

#### Step 4: Reject the Intuitive Story Trap

If an additional condition has to be met, no matter how plausible it sounds, it will become less, not more, likely. When evaluating options, strip away the narrative and compare the bare probabilities. In the Chris example, option B seems more vivid but is statistically less likely than option A. Forget about left brains and right brains — the real difference is between intuitive and conscious thinking.
#### Best Practices

- ✅ With important decisions, remember that, at the intuitive level, we have a soft spot for plausible stories.

#### Keep In Mind

- With important decisions, remember that, at the intuitive level, we have a soft spot for plausible stories.
- Remember: if an additional condition has to be met, no matter how plausible it sounds, it will become less, not more, likely.

#### Security & Safety Notes

- We are easy prey for the conjunction fallacy because we have an innate attraction to ‘harmonious’ or ‘plausible’ stories.
- The more convincingly, impressively or vividly Chris the aid worker is portrayed, the greater the risk of false reasoning.

#### Common Pitfalls

- The conjunction fallacy is at play when such a subset seems larger than the entire set – which by deﬁnition cannot be the case.
- We are easy prey for the conjunction fallacy because we have an innate attraction to ‘harmonious’ or ‘plausible’ stories.
- The more convincingly, impressively or vividly Chris the aid worker is portrayed, the greater the risk of false reasoning.

### Lesson 42: Framing

#### Examples

##### Example 1: Illustrative Example

Another example: researchers presented a group of people with two kinds of meat, ‘99% fat free’ and ‘1% fat’, and asked them to choose which was healthier. Can you guess which they picked? Bingo: respondents ranked the ﬁrst type of meat as healthier, even though both were identical. Next came the choice between ‘98% fat free’ and ‘1% fat’. Again, most respondents chose the ﬁrst option – despite its higher fat content. Glossing is a popular type of framing. Under its rules, a tumbling share price becomes ‘correction’. An overpaid acquisition price is branded ‘goodwill’.

#### Steps

#### Step 1: Test How Framing Affects You

In conclusion: realise that whatever you communicate contains some element of framing, and that every fact – even if you hear it from a trusted friend or read it in a reputable newspaper – is subject to this effect, too.

#### Best Practices

- ✅ In conclusion: realise that whatever you communicate contains some element of framing, and that every fact is subject to this effect.
- ✅ When evaluating options, rewrite them in different frames to see whether your preference changes.
- ❌ Glossing is a popular type of framing: a tumbling share price becomes 'correction', an overpaid acquisition price is branded 'goodwill'.

#### Keep In Mind

- In conclusion: realise that whatever you communicate contains some element of framing, and that every fact – even if you hear it from a trusted friend or read it in a reputable newspaper – is subject to this effect, too.

#### Security & Safety Notes

- It became really interesting when the same options were reframed. ‘Option A kills 400 people’, ‘Option B offers a 33% chance that no one will die, and a 66% chance that all 600 will die.’ This time, only a fraction of respondents chose A and the majority picked B.
- Another example: researchers presented a group of people with two kinds of meat, ‘99% fat free’ and ‘1% fat’, and asked them to choose which was healthier.

#### Common Pitfalls

- In every management course, a problem magically transforms into an ‘opportunity’ or a ‘challenge’.

---

### Lesson 43: Action Bias

#### Examples

In penalty shootouts, goalkeepers dive left or right two-thirds of the time even though the ball goes straight down the middle just as often – they'd rather look active than stand still and watch it sail past, as Israeli researcher Michael Bar-Eli discovered. Young police officers at nightclubs rush in immediately while experienced ones wait, resulting in fewer injuries. New investors compensate for uncertainty with hyperactivity, and doctors facing undiagnosable patients choose to intervene rather than wait, even though inaction would be wiser. As Charlie Munger puts it: 'We've got discipline in avoiding just doing any damn thing just because you can't stand inactivity.'

#### Steps

#### Step 1: Identify When You Are Being Liked

Society at large still prefers rash action to a sensible wait-and-see strategy.

#### Step 2: Resist the Urge to Act

In uncertain situations, people often feel pressure to act rather than wait, even when inaction would be more effective.

#### Best Practices

- ✅ Society at large still prefers rash action to a sensible wait-and-see strategy.

#### Keep In Mind

You will encounter action bias whenever you feel compelled to intervene in a situation even when waiting would produce a better outcome, such as a goalkeeper diving instead of staying centred.

#### Security & Safety Notes

- This bias can create safety risks when hasty action replaces careful assessment. Young police officers at nightclubs rush in immediately while experienced ones wait, resulting in fewer injuries.

#### Common Pitfalls

- So, though the situation does not warrant dramatic action, if a situation is unclear, hold back until you can assess your options. ‘All of humanity’s problems stem from man’s inability to sit quietly in a room alone,’ wrote Blaise Pascal.
- See also Omission Bias (ch. 44); Overthinking (ch. 90); Procrastination (ch. 85); The It’ll-Get-Worse-Before-It-Gets-Better Fallacy (ch. 12); Inability to Close Doors (ch. 68)

---

### Lesson 44: Omission Bias

#### Examples

##### Example 1: Hypothetical Scenario

Suppose you are the head of the Federal Drug Administration. You must decide whether or not to approve a drug for the terminally ill. The medication has immediate risks: it causes fatal reactions in 20% of patients, but saves the remaining 80% within days of administration. What do you decide? Most would withhold approval. To them, waving through a drug that takes out every ﬁfth person is a worse act than failing to administer the cure to the other 80% of patients. It is an absurd decision, and a perfect example of the omission bias. Suppose that you are aware of the bias and decide to approve the drug in the name of reason and decency.

#### Steps

#### Step 1: Evaluate Action and Inaction Equally

In such cases, we tend to prefer inaction; its results seem more anodyne.

#### Step 2: Act Despite the Omission Bias

Such thinking also explains why parents feel it is perfectly acceptable not to vaccinate their children, even though vaccination discernibly reduces the risk of catching the disease.

#### Step 3: Notice Passive Harm

Failing to insulate your house is more acceptable than burning the spared fuel for your own amusement.

#### Best Practices

- ✅ In such cases, we tend to prefer inaction; its results seem more anodyne.
- ✅ Such thinking also explains why parents feel it is perfectly acceptable not to vaccinate their children, even though vaccination discernibly reduces the risk of catching the disease.
- ❌ Considering the options rationally, it's obvious that both active harm and passive harm are equally reprehensible, resulting as they do in death for your companions.

#### Keep In Mind

You will encounter omission bias whenever you feel that actively causing harm is worse than passively allowing the same harm — even when the outcomes are identical.

#### Security & Safety Notes

- Considering the options rationally, it’s obvious that both are equally reprehensible, resulting as they do in death for your companions.
- The medication has immediate risks: it causes fatal reactions in 20% of patients, but saves the remaining 80% within days of administration.

#### Common Pitfalls

- It is an absurd decision, and a perfect example of the omission bias.
- Such thinking also explains why parents feel it is perfectly acceptable not to vaccinate their children, even though vaccination discernibly reduces the risk of catching the disease.
- Of course, there is also a very small risk of getting sick from the vaccine.

---

### Lesson 45: Self-Serving Bias

#### Examples

CEOs attribute successful years to their brilliant decisions and tireless efforts, but miserable years to exchange rates, government interference, and malicious Chinese trade practices. Students who get A's credit their intelligence and hard work; those who fail blame unfair tests. Investors applaud themselves for gains but blame 'the market' for losses. Richard Fuld, CEO of Lehman Brothers, still calls himself 'master of the universe' while blaming government for the bank's 2008 collapse. Students inflate their SAT scores by 50 points a year later without lying, roommates collectively claim 320% of household chores, and married couples both believe they contribute more than half to the relationship.

#### Steps



#### Step 1: Seek Honest Feedback

Do you have friends who tell you the truth — no holds barred? If so, consider yourself lucky. If not, do you have at least one enemy? Good. Invite him or her over for coffee and ask for an honest opinion about your strengths and weaknesses. You will be forever grateful you did.

#### Step 2: Question Your Attribution

When something goes well, ask yourself: would I credit external factors if the outcome had been bad? When something goes badly, ask: would I credit myself if the outcome had been good? This forces you to break the automatic pattern of attributing success to skill and failure to circumstance.

#### Step 3: Beware the 'Master of the Universe' Trap

CEOs like Richard Fuld blame government inaction for their bank's collapse while still calling themselves 'master of the universe.' If you find yourself explaining away failures with external forces while taking full credit for successes, you are caught in the self-serving bias. Recognise it and adjust your self-assessment accordingly.

#### Best Practices

- ✅ If you got an A, you were solely responsible; the top grade reflected your intelligence, hard work and skill.
- ✅ If you failed, examine whether external factors played a role — but also ask whether you would have blamed external factors if you had succeeded.
- ❌ CEOs attribute successful years to their brilliant decisions and tireless efforts, but miserable years to exchange rates, government interference, and malicious Chinese trade practices.

#### Keep In Mind

You will encounter self-serving bias whenever you credit your successes to skill and hard work but blame your failures on bad luck or external forces.

#### Security & Safety Notes

- If you got an A, you were solely responsible; the top grade reﬂected your intelligence, hard work and skill.
- So why do we attribute success to our own skill and ascribe failure to other factors?

#### Common Pitfalls

- If you got an A, you were solely responsible; the top grade reﬂected your intelligence, hard work and skill.
- But beware: in a modern world with hidden risks, the self-serving bias can quickly lead to catastrophe.
- Richard Fuld, the self-titled ‘master of the universe’, would likely endorse this.

---

### Lesson 46: Hedonic Treadmill

#### Examples

##### Example 1: Anecdote

In other words, whoever has no innate afﬁnity for commuting will suffer every day – twice a day. Anyhow, the moral of the story is that the dream villa had an overall negative effect on my friend’s happiness. Others fare no better: Research suggests that the happiness boost from career changes tends to fade within months, returning individuals to their baseline level of satisfaction. The same goes for people who buy the latest Porsche. Psychologists call this phenomenon the hedonic treadmill: the tendency for people to return to a relatively stable level of happiness after major life events. So how do negative events affect us – say, a spinal cord injury or the loss of a friend? Here, we also overestimate the duration and intensity of future emotions. For example, when a relationship ends it feels like life will never be the same.

#### Steps



#### Step 1: Avoid Things You Cannot Grow Accustomed To

Scientifically rubber-stamped advice: avoid negative things that you cannot grow accustomed to, such as commuting, noise, or chronic stress. A one-hour daily commute is a major source of discontent that people never get used to — no matter how long they do it.

#### Step 2: Expect Only Short-Term Happiness from Material Things

Material gains like cars, houses, and bonuses produce temporary happiness increases that typically diminish within six to twelve months. The dream villa will feel like your student apartment again within six months. Buy things for their utility, not for the lasting joy they promise.

#### Step 3: Invest in Experiences, Autonomy, and Friendships

Long-lasting positive effects come from what you actively do: follow your passions even if you must forfeit a portion of your income, invest in friendships, and seek as much free time and autonomy as possible. Professional status achieves long-lasting happiness — as long as you don't change peer groups at the same time.

#### Best Practices

- ✅ Use these research-backed principles to improve the quality of your decisions: 1) Avoid negative things that you cannot grow accustomed to, such as commuting, noise or chronic stress. 2) Expect only short-term happiness from material things, such as cars, houses, lottery winnings, bonuses and prizes.

#### Keep In Mind

You will encounter the hedonic treadmill whenever a dream villa, a promotion, or a new Porsche fails to deliver the lasting happiness you expected.

#### Security & Safety Notes

- So how do negative events affect us – say, a spinal cord injury or the loss of a friend?
- For example, when a relationship ends it feels like life will never be the same.

#### Common Pitfalls

- This may sound tolerable, but research suggests that commuting by car can be a source of discontent and stress, and people hardly ever get used to it.
- In other words, whoever has no innate afﬁnity for commuting will suffer every day – twice a day.
- Psychologists call this phenomenon the hedonic treadmill: the tendency for people to return to a relatively stable level of happiness after major life events.

---

### Lesson 47: Self-Selection Bias

#### Examples

##### Example 1: Hypothetical Scenario

If this doesn’t seem right, imagine that you are travelling at near the speed of light. In this case, you would spend 99.99% (not 10%) of your total journey time waiting and cursing in front of red traffic lights. Whenever we complain about bad luck, we must be wary of the so-called self- selection bias. My male friends often gripe about there being an underrepresentation of women in their companies, and my female friends groan that theirs have an underrepresentation of men. This has nothing to do with bad luck: the grumblers form part of the sample. The probability is high that a man will work in a mostly male industry. Ditto for women. On a grander scale, if you live in a country with a large proportion of men or women (such as China or Russia, respectively), you are likely to form part of the bigger group and accordingly feel hard done by.

#### Steps

#### Step 1: Recognize Self-Selection in Your Sample

Travelling from Philadelphia up to New York, I got stuck in a trafﬁc jam. ‘Why is it always me?’ I groaned.

#### Step 2: Question Miraculous Observations

Do I always pick the worst lines at the bank, post ofﬁce and grocery store?

#### Step 3: Account for Sample Selection in Surveys

On average, one out of the ten will always be red and the others green.

#### Best Practices

- ✅ Travelling from Philadelphia up to New York, I got stuck in a traffic jam. 'Why is it always me?' I groaned — until I realised that I only notice the red lights, not the green ones.
- ✅ Do I always pick the worst lines at the bank, post office and grocery store? On average, one out of ten will always be red and the others green.
- ❌ My male friends often gripe about there being an underrepresentation of women in their companies, and my female friends groan that theirs have an underrepresentation of men — but the grumblers form part of the sample.

#### Keep In Mind

You will encounter self-selection bias whenever you complain that your line is always the slowest at the grocery store, or that your gender is underrepresented in your industry.

#### Security & Safety Notes

- This bias mainly influences personal perception and workplace dynamics, leading people to make inaccurate assessments about fairness or representation based on self-selected samples.

#### Common Pitfalls

- On a grander scale, if you live in a country with a large proportion of men or women (such as China or Russia, respectively), you are likely to form part of the bigger group and accordingly feel hard done by.
- See also Alternative Paths (ch. 39); Feature-Positive Effect (ch. 95); Swimmer’s Body Illusion (ch. 2)

---

### Lesson 48: Association Bias

#### Examples

Kevin wore green polka-dot boxer shorts to three successful board presentations and concluded they were lucky. He bought a $10,000 engagement ring because the stunning saleswoman made him associate the ring with a dazzling future wife, and he cancels doctor appointments on hot days because two bad diagnoses happened to occur on warm afternoons. Pavlov's dogs began salivating at the sound of a bell that had been paired with food. Coke ads never show frowning faces. A travelling salesman named George Foster developed a lasting fear of doorbells after a gas explosion at a vacant house. Mark Twain warned: 'We should be careful to get out of an experience only the wisdom that is in it – and stop there.'

#### Steps

#### Step 1: Recognize Association in Coincidences

1) When two events occur together, ask whether there is a real causal link or whether the pairing was coincidental. 2) Be wary of drawing conclusions from a small number of coincidences — Kevin's warm-day diagnoses could easily be random. 3) Remember that correlation does not imply causation: Pavlov's dogs learned to associate the bell with food, but the bell itself had no nutritional value. 4) Strip away the emotional or narrative associations before making decisions based on them.

#### Best Practices

- ✅ Since then, Kevin has always felt uncomfortable on very warm days — even though the heat had nothing to do with the bad diagnosis.
- ✅ When you associate two events that occurred together, ask whether there is a causal link or whether the pairing was coincidental.
- ❌ Kevin wore green polka-dot boxer shorts to three successful board presentations and concluded they were lucky.

#### Keep In Mind

You will encounter association bias whenever you avoid warm days because a bad diagnosis happened on one, or when you wear 'lucky' clothing to important events.

#### Security & Safety Notes

- The bell was also damaged, so when George Foster pressed it, it created a spark and the house exploded.

#### Common Pitfalls

- Once the problem was his appendix, which was promptly removed.

---

### Lesson 49: Beginner’s Luck

#### Examples

Kevin wore the same outfit to three successful meetings before a presentation did not go as planned. Company A successfully acquired smaller firms B, C, and D before a massive acquisition of E turned into a disaster. During the dot-com boom, dentists, lawyers, and teachers quit their jobs to flip houses or trade internet stocks, convinced early profits reflected skill rather than a rising market. Napoleon and Hitler both launched Russian campaigns buoyed by earlier small victories. The author's first novel was accepted by one publisher, making him feel like a genius – until ten other publishers rejected it, disproving the notion entirely.

#### Steps

#### Step 1: Beware Beginner's Luck Traps

Beginner’s luck can be devastating, so guard against misconceptions by treating your theories as a scientist would: try to disprove them.

#### Step 2: Question Early Success

As soon as my ﬁrst novel, Thirty-five, was ready to go, I sent it to a single publisher, where it was promptly accepted.

#### Best Practices

- ✅ Beginner's luck can be devastating, so guard against misconceptions by treating your theories as a scientist would: try to disprove them.
- ✅ Convinced of their above-average skills, these amateurs increase the stakes — but they will soon get a sobering wake-up call when the probabilities 'normalise'.
- ❌ The acquisitions prove a success, and the directors believe they have a real skill for acquisitions — until the pattern reverses.

#### Keep In Mind

You will encounter beginner's luck whenever an early success leads you to overestimate your skill and increase your stakes, only to face a sobering reversal when probabilities normalise.

#### Security & Safety Notes

- Convinced of their above-average skills, these amateurs increase the stakes – but they will soon get a sobering wake-up call when the probabilities ‘normalise’.
- The acquisitions prove a success, and the directors believe they have a real skill for acquisitions.

#### Common Pitfalls

- We now come to a particularly tricky branch of the association bias: creating a (false) link with the past.
- See also Survivorship Bias (ch. 1); Self-Serving Bias (ch. 45); Association Bias (ch. 48); False Causality (ch. 37); Illusion of Skill (ch. 94)

### Lesson 50: Cognitive Dissonance

#### Examples

Aesop's fox, unable to reach the grapes, concluded they were probably sour anyway. A car buyer who regretted a purchase with a loud engine convinced himself the noise was actually a safety feature. In Festinger and Carlsmith's 1959 experiment, students paid only $1 to lie about a boring task rated it as statistically significantly more enjoyable than those paid $20 – the small payment wasn't enough to justify outright lying, so they convinced themselves the work wasn't so bad. The author tried to persuade a friend that his losing stock still had 'more potential overall,' and job applicants often convince themselves they never really wanted the position after being rejected.

#### Steps

#### Step 1: Remember the Key Point

Quite the reverse, in fact: I distinctly remember trying to convince a friend that, though the stock was experiencing teething problems, it still had more potential overall.

#### Best Practices

- ✅ When you make a difficult choice between two options, remember that the rejected option will seem more attractive afterward — and that this feeling does not mean you made the wrong decision.
- ✅ He can resolve this conflict in one of three ways: A) by somehow getting at the grapes, B) by admitting that his skills are insufficient, or C) by retrospectively reinterpreting what happened.
- ❌ A car buyer who regretted a purchase with a loud engine convinced himself the noise was actually a safety feature.

#### Keep In Mind

You will encounter cognitive dissonance whenever a fox decides the grapes he cannot reach are probably sour anyway, or when you convince yourself that a rejected job offer was never that appealing.

#### Security & Safety Notes

- The fox can resolve this conﬂict in one of three ways: A) by somehow getting at the grapes, B) by admitting that his skills are insufﬁcient, or C) by retrospectively reinterpreting what happened.
- So you tell yourself that a loud engine and awkward seats are great safety features that will prevent you from falling asleep at the wheel.

#### Common Pitfalls

- Quite the reverse, in fact: I distinctly remember trying to convince a friend that, though the stock was experiencing teething problems, it still had more potential overall.

### Lesson 51: Hyperbolic Discounting

#### Examples

##### Example 1: Experiment

In the 60s, Walter Mischel conducted a famous experiment on delayed gratiﬁcation. You can ﬁnd a wonderful video of this on YouTube by typing in ‘marshmallow experiment’. In it, a group of four-year-olds were each given a marshmallow. They can either eat it straight away or wait a couple of minutes and receive a second. Amazingly, some children wait. Even more amazingly, Mischel found that the capacity for delayed gratiﬁcation is a reliable indicator of future career success. Patience is indeed a virtue. The older we get and the more self-control we build up, the more easily we can delay rewards.

#### Steps

#### Step 1: Delay Gratification Consciously

Second question: would you prefer $1,000 today cash on the table or $1,100 in a month?

#### Step 2: Restrict Exposure

You can train rats as much as you like; they’re never going to give up a piece of cheese today to get two pieces tomorrow.

#### Step 3: Confront the Hyperbolic Discounting Bias

In conclusion: though instantaneous reward is incredibly tempting, hyperbolic discounting is still a ﬂaw.

#### Best Practices

- ✅ The more power we gain over our impulses, the better we can avoid this trap.

#### Keep In Mind

You will encounter hyperbolic discounting whenever you choose a smaller immediate reward over a larger delayed one, even when waiting costs you nothing and pays you more.

#### Security & Safety Notes

- You know the saying: ‘Live each day as if it were your last.’ It features at least three times in every lifestyle magazine, and has a slot in every self-help manual’s standard repertoire, too.
- A wise choice, since the interest will compensate you generously for any risks you face by waiting the extra weeks.

#### Common Pitfalls

- A wise choice, since the interest will compensate you generously for any risks you face by waiting the extra weeks.

### Lesson 52: ‘Because’ Justification

#### Examples

##### Example 1: Experiment

Reassuringly cheerful signs along the road announced: ‘We’re renovating the highway for you!’ The jam reminded me of an experiment conducted by the Harvard psychologist Ellen Langer in the 1970s. For this, she went into a library and waited at a photocopier until a line had formed. Then she approached the ﬁrst in line and said: ‘Excuse me, I have ﬁve pages. May I use the Xerox machine?’ Her success rate was 60 per cent. She repeated the experiment, this time giving a reason: ‘Excuse me. I have ﬁve pages. May I use the Xerox machine, because I’m in a rush?’ In almost all cases (94 per cent), she was allowed to go ahead. This is understandable: if people are in a hurry, you often let them cut in to the front of the line.

##### Example 2: Anecdote

No: today, higher purposes and the story behind the story are all-important; for example: ‘We want our shoes to revolutionise the market’ (whatever that means). ‘Better arch support for a better world!’ (whatever that means). Zappo’s uses vague marketing language (whatever that means). If the stock market rises or falls by half a per cent, you will never hear the true cause from stock market commentators – that it is white noise, the culmination of an inﬁnite number of market movements. No: people want a palpable reason and the commentator is happy to select one. Whatever explanation he utters will be meaningless – with frequent blame applied to the pronouncements of Federal Reserve Bank presidents. If someone asks why you have yet to complete a task, it’s best to say: ‘Because I haven’t got around to it yet.’ It’s a pathetic excuse (had you done so, the conversation wouldn’t be taking place), but it usually does the trick without the need to scramble for more plausible reasons. One day I watched my wife carefully separating black laundry from blue. As far as I know, this effort isn’t necessary.

#### Steps

#### Step 1: Demand Real Reasons

Such logic has managed to keep my clothes run-free for three years. ‘Why do you do that?’ I asked. ‘Because I prefer to wash them separately.’ For me, a perfectly ﬁne answer.

#### Best Practices

- ✅ Such logic has managed to keep my clothes run-free for three years. ‘Why do you do that?’ I asked. ‘Because I prefer to wash them separately.’ For me, a perfectly ﬁne answer.
- ✅ Never leave home without ‘because’.

#### Keep In Mind

You will encounter the 'because' justification whenever a simple reason — even a meaningless one — dramatically increases compliance, as Ellen Langer's photocopier experiment demonstrated.

#### Security & Safety Notes

- Flight 1234 is delayed by three hours.’ Wonderful.
- Other airlines have the decency to announce: ‘Flight 5678 is delayed by three hours due to operational reasons.’ A throwaway reason if there ever was one, but enough to appease passengers.

#### Common Pitfalls

- See also Cognitive Dissonance (ch. 50); Story Bias (ch. 13); Fallacy of the Single Cause (ch. 97)

### Lesson 53: Decision Fatigue

#### Examples

##### Example 1: Experiment

At the end of the experiment I’ll give you one item you can take home.’ They were led to believe that their choices would inﬂuence which item they got to keep. To the second group, he said: ‘Write down what you think about each item, and I’ll pick one and give it to you at the end.’ Immediately thereafter, he asked each student to put their hand in ice-cold water and hold it there for as long as possible. In psychology, this is a classic method to measure willpower or self-discipline; if you have little or none, you yank your hand back out of the water very quickly. The result: the deciders pulled their hands out of the icy water much sooner than the non-deciders did. The intensive decision-making had drained their willpower – an effect conﬁrmed in many other experiments. Making decisions is exhausting. Anyone who has ever conﬁgured a laptop online or researched a long trip – ﬂight, hotels, activities, restaurants, weather – knows this well: after all the comparing, considering and choosing, you are exhausted. Science calls this decision fatigue.

#### Steps

#### Step 1: Reduce Decision Load

He told the ﬁrst group: ‘I’m going to show you sets containing two random items and each time you have to decide which you prefer.

#### Step 2: Document Your Thinking

To the second group, Roy Baumeister said: ‘Write down what you think about each item, and I’ll pick one and give it to you at the end.’ Immediately thereafter, Roy Baumeister asked each student to put their hand in ice-cold water and hold it there for as long as possible.

#### Best Practices

- ✅ Roy Baumeister told the first group: 'I'm going to show you sets containing two random items and each time you have to decide which you prefer.'
- ✅ To the second group, Roy Baumeister said: 'Write down what you think about each item, and I'll pick one and give it to you at the end.'
- ❌ The intensive decision-making had drained their willpower: the deciders pulled their hands out of the icy water much sooner than the non-deciders did.

#### Keep In Mind

You will encounter decision fatigue whenever you make poor impulse purchases after a long day of choices, or when judges hand down harsher sentences later in the day.

#### Security & Safety Notes

- Case 1 (scheduled for 8.50a.m.): an Arab sentenced to 30 months in prison for fraud.
- Case 4 (scheduled for 4.35p.m.): an Arab sentenced to 30 months for fraud.

#### Common Pitfalls

- Decision fatigue is perilous: as a consumer, you become more susceptible to advertising messages and impulse buys.
- However, they struck out applications 3 and 4 because they does not summon summon enough energy to risk the consequences of an early release.

### Lesson 54: Contagion Bias

#### Examples

##### Example 1: Real-World Story

When I attended one of her dinner parties in Paris recently, the gilded goblets had pride of place on the dining table. ‘Are these from Lafayette?’ one person asked. ‘No, they are from Saddam Hussein,’ she said candidly. A horriﬁed guest spat his wine back into the glass and began to splutter uncontrollably. I had to contribute: ‘You realise about a billion molecules you’ve already shared with Saddam, simply by breathing?’ I asked. ‘About a billion per breath.’ His cough got even worse. See also Association Bias (ch. 48); Affect Heuristic (ch. 66)

#### Steps



#### Step 1: Test Your Own Rationality

Ask yourself: would you put on a freshly laundered sweater that Hitler had once worn? There isn't a single molecule of Hitler's sweat on it, and touching it doesn't endorse Hitler in any way — yet the prospect still puts you off. This emotional reaction is difficult to override, even for people who consider themselves quite rational.

#### Step 2: Recognise the Invisible Connection

The contagion bias describes how we are incapable of ignoring the connection we feel to certain items — be they from long ago or only indirectly related. A gilded wine glass from Saddam Hussein's palace makes a guest spit his wine back into it, as if a mystic force were at work. Acknowledge that this bias exists in you, too.

#### Step 3: Separate the Object from Its History

When you feel an irrational aversion to an object because of its history, remind yourself that the object itself is neutral. The sweater, the wine glass, or the photo pinned to a dartboard have no actual power — only the associations you project onto them matter.

#### Best Practices

- ✅ I had to contribute: 'You realise about a billion molecules you've already shared with Saddam, simply by breathing?' I asked. 'About a billion per breath.' His cough got even worse.
- ✅ Even those who consider themselves quite rational have a hard time completely banishing the belief in mysterious forces (me included).
- ❌ Riddling a picture with darts does no harm to the person in it, but nevertheless the subjects' hesitation was palpable.

#### Keep In Mind

- I had to contribute: ‘You realise about a billion molecules you’ve already shared with Saddam, simply by breathing?’ I asked. ‘About a billion per breath.’ His cough got even worse.

#### Security & Safety Notes

- Riddling a picture with darts does no harm to the person in it, but nevertheless the subjects’ hesitation was palpable.

#### Common Pitfalls

- Even those who consider themselves quite rational have a hard time completely banishing the belief in mysterious forces (me included).
