### Lesson 55: The Problem with Averages

#### Examples

##### Example 1: Illustrative Example

Another example: the average amount of UV rays you are exposed to on a June day is not harmful to your health. But if you were to spend the entire summer in a darkened ofﬁce, then ﬂy to Barbados and lie in the sun without sunscreen for a week solid, you would have a problem – even though, on average over the summer, you were not getting more UV light than someone who was outside regularly. All this is quite straightforward, and you may already be aware of it. For example, you drink one glass of red wine at dinner every evening. That’s not a health issue. Some doctors recommend it. But if you drink no alcohol the entire year and on Dec 31 you gulp 356 glasses, which is equivalent to sixty bottles, you will have a problem, although the average over the year is the same. Here’s the update: in a complex world, distribution is becoming more and more irregular.

##### Example 2: Illustrative Example

To use just the ﬁnal example: a handful of actors take home more than $10 million per year, while thousands and thousands live on the breadline. Would you advise your son or daughter to get into acting since the average wage is pretty decent? Hopefully not – wrong reason. In conclusion: if someone uses the word ‘average’, think twice. Try to work out the underlying distribution. If a single anomaly has almost no inﬂuence on the set, the concept is still worthwhile. However, when extreme cases dominate (such as the Bill Gates phenomenon), we should discount the term ‘average’. We should all take stock from novelist William Gibson: ‘The future is already here – it’s just not very evenly distributed.’ See also Base-Rate Neglect (ch. 28); Simple Logic (ch. 63); Regression to Mean (ch. 19); Neglect of Probability (ch. 26); Gambler’s Fallacy (ch. 29)

#### Steps

#### Step 1: Demand the Full Distribution

In conclusion: if someone uses the word ‘average’, think twice.

#### Best Practices

- ✅ In conclusion: if someone uses the word 'average', think twice. Try to work out the underlying distribution.
- ✅ If a single anomaly has almost no influence on the set, the concept is still worthwhile. However, when extreme cases dominate, we should discount the term 'average'.
- ❌ Averages can be misleading because they frequently hide the actual spread of values, particularly when extreme cases dominate the dataset.

#### Keep In Mind

You will encounter the problem with averages whenever a handful of actors take home more than $10 million per year while thousands live on the breadline, yet the average wage looks decent.

#### Security & Safety Notes

- Averages can be misleading because they frequently obscure the actual distribution of values, especially when extreme cases dominate.
- Another example: the average amount of UV rays you are exposed to on a June day is not harmful to your health.

#### Common Pitfalls

- Averages can be misleading because they frequently obscure the actual distribution of values, especially when extreme cases dominate.
- But if you were to spend the entire summer in a darkened ofﬁce, then ﬂy to Barbados and lie in the sun without sunscreen for a week solid, you would have a problem – even though, on average over the summer, you were not getting more UV light than someone who was outside regularly.
- But if you drink no alcohol the entire year and on Dec 31 you gulp 356 glasses, which is equivalent to sixty bottles, you will have a problem, although the average over the year is the same.

### Lesson 56: Motivation Crowding

#### Examples

A friend from Connecticut was helping the author move fragile antiques to New York, but when the author slipped him $50 as thanks, the payment undermined the good deed and tainted their friendship. In the Swiss village of Wolfenschiessen, support for storing nuclear waste dropped from 50.8% to 24.6% when residents were offered $5,000 each – the money was perceived as a bribe that cheapened civic spirit. Daycare centres that introduced late fees to discourage parental tardiness actually saw lateness increase, because the fees transformed an interpersonal relationship into a monetary transaction that essentially legitimised being late.

#### Steps

#### Step 1: Avoid Crowding Out Intrinsic Motivation

Surprisingly, 50.8% were in favour of the proposal.

#### Step 2: Use Incentives Only Where Passion Is Absent

The team carried out the survey a second time, but this time they mentioned a hypothetical reward of $5,000 for each townsperson, paid for by Swiss taxpayers, if they were to accept the proposal.

#### Step 3: Pay Children a Fixed Allowance

The three stories illustrate one thing: money does not always motivate.

#### Best Practices

- ❌ If you want your kids to do their homework, practise musical instruments, or even mow the lawn once in a while, do not reach for your wallet.

#### Keep In Mind

You will encounter motivation crowding whenever a small monetary reward undermines an intrinsic motivation, such as when daycare fees increased tardiness instead of reducing it.

#### Security & Safety Notes

- If you suddenly introduce a bonus system – let’s say a small salary increase for every donation secured – motivation crowding will commence.

#### Common Pitfalls

- Daycare workers face the same issue the world over: parents collecting their children after closing time.
- They can hardly put the last remaining children in taxis or leave them on the kerb.

---

### Lesson 57: Twaddle Tendency

#### Examples

Miss Teen South Carolina, asked why one-fifth of Americans couldn't locate their country on a world map, delivered an incoherent answer about 'the Iraq everywhere like such as.' German philosopher Jürgen Habermas wrote sentences like 'there is certainly no necessity that this increasingly reflexive transmission of cultural traditions be associated with subject-centred reason and future-oriented historical consciousness' that sound profound but say nothing. The author once found Jacques Derrida's writings fascinating until realizing they were just empty chatter. Former GE CEO Jack Welch said: 'You would not believe how difficult it is to be simple and clear. People are afraid they may be seen as a simpleton. In reality, just the opposite is true.'

#### Steps

#### Step 1: Know When to Stay Silent

In reality, just the opposite is true.’ In conclusion: verbal expression is the mirror of the mind.

#### Best Practices

- ✅ In reality, just the opposite is true.' In conclusion: verbal expression is the mirror of the mind.
- ✅ If used in conjunction with the authority bias it can be especially dangerous as we are willing to accept the words without questioning them.
- ❌ Top German philosopher and sociologist Jürgen Habermas in Between Facts and Norms wrote sentences that sound profound but say nothing.

#### Keep In Mind

You will encounter twaddle tendency whenever you hear jargon that sounds profound but conveys no meaning, such as Miss Teen South Carolina's incoherent answer about the Iraq 'everywhere like such as.'

#### Security & Safety Notes

- If used in conjunction with the authority bias it can be especially dangerous as we are willing to accept the words without questioning them.

#### Common Pitfalls

- To the extent that we become aware of the intersubjective constitution of freedom, the possessive-individualist illusion of autonomy as self-ownership disintegrates.’ Ring any bells?
- For the beauty queen, the smokescreen strategy failed spectacularly.
- If used in conjunction with the authority bias it can be especially dangerous as we are willing to accept the words without questioning them.

### Lesson 58: Will Rogers Phenomenon

#### Examples

You manage two money managers: A handles ultra-high-net-worth clients, B handles merely rich ones. Moving a sizeable client from A to B improves both averages without acquiring a single new client. In a hedge fund trio with sensational, mediocre, and miserable returns, transferring A's weakest performers to B and C makes all three look healthier while the combined value stays unchanged. An auto franchise transfers salesman number 4 from branch B to branch A, raising A's average from 2 to 2.5 cars per week and B's from 5 to 5.5 – pure illusion. In medicine, new screening techniques detect smaller tumours, shifting patients into earlier stages and inflating survival rates even when nothing has actually improved.

#### Steps



#### Step 1: Be Alert to Average-Inflating Switcheroos

When you hear that an average has risen — in a company, department, country, or product line — ask whether the improvement came from actually getting better or from moving items between groups. Transferring a mid-performer from a strong group to a weak one raises both averages without adding any real value.

#### Step 2: Demand the Combined Figure

Always look at the total or combined figure, not just the subgroup averages. Three hedge funds that all look healthier after a reallocation have not improved one cent in total worth. The Will Rogers phenomenon creates an impressive illusion — but the underlying total is unchanged.

#### Step 3: Watch for Stage Migration in Medical Statistics

New screening techniques that detect smaller tumours shift patients into earlier stages, inflating survival rates for those stages even when no actual improvement has occurred. Treat changes in grouped statistics with scepticism until you have verified that the total population outcome has genuinely improved.

#### Best Practices

- ✅ Such switcheroo strategies don't change anything overall, but they create an impressive illusion.
- ✅ In no time, all three funds look much healthier — but the combined value stays unchanged.
- ❌ The result: patients who were erroneously diagnosed as healthy before are now counted as stage-one patients, inflating survival rates even when nothing has actually improved.

#### Keep In Mind

You will encounter the Will Rogers phenomenon whenever moving a mediocre performer from one group to another makes both groups look better — even though nothing has actually improved.

#### Security & Safety Notes

- In no time, all three funds look much healthier.
- The result: patients who were erroneously diagnosed as healthy before are now counted as stage-one patients.

#### Common Pitfalls

- Such switcheroo strategies don’t change anything overall, but they create an impressive illusion.

---

### Lesson 59: Information Bias

#### Examples

##### Example 1: San Diego vs. San Antonio (Information Bias)

Consider this question: which city has more inhabitants – San Diego or San Antonio? Gerd Gigerenzer of the Max Planck Institute in Germany put this question to students in the Universities of Chicago and Munich. Sixty-two per cent of Chicago students guessed right: San Diego has more. But, astonishingly, every single German student answered correctly. The reason: all of them had heard of San Diego, but not necessarily of San Antonio, so they opted for the more familiar city. For the Chicagoans, however, both cities were household names. They had more information and it misled them. Or, consider the hundreds of thousands of economists – in the service of banks, think tanks, hedge funds and governments – and all the white papers they have published from 2005 to 2007, the vast library of research reports and mathematical models.

#### Steps

#### Step 1: Actively Challenge

Do your best to get by with the bare facts.

#### Best Practices

- ✅ Do your best to get by with the bare facts.
- ✅ The greatest obstacle to discovery is not ignorance — it is the illusion of knowledge.
- ❌ For the Chicagoans, however, both San Diego and San Antonio were household names. They had more information and it misled them.

#### Keep In Mind

You will encounter information bias whenever you believe that more information will automatically lead to a better decision, or when you request unnecessary test results that cannot change the diagnosis.

#### Security & Safety Notes

- Boorstin put it right: ‘The greatest obstacle to discovery is not ignorance – it is the illusion of knowledge.’ And next time you are confronted by a rival, consider killing him – not with kindness but with reams of data and analysis.

#### Common Pitfalls

- Jonathan Baron from the University of Pennsylvania asked physicians the following question: a patient presents symptoms that indicate with a probability of 80% that he is suffering from disease A.
- Boorstin put it right: ‘The greatest obstacle to discovery is not ignorance – it is the illusion of knowledge.’ And next time you are confronted by a rival, consider killing him – not with kindness but with reams of data and analysis.
- See also Overthinking (ch. 90); News Illusion (ch. 99); Base-Rate Neglect (ch. 28)

### Lesson 60: Effort Justification

#### Examples

A U.S. Army paratrooper receives his pin pounded through his chest and treasures it above all other awards, displaying it decades later in a specially made frame. Mark singlehandedly restores a rusty Harley-Davidson over years of weekends and holidays, nearly destroying his marriage, and refuses to sell it even for double its value. MBA schools work students to exhaustion, ensuring they'll later deem the qualification essential simply because it demanded so much. The IKEA effect makes self-assembled furniture feel more valuable than designer pieces, and in the 1950s, housewives rejected instant cake mixes until manufacturers made preparation slightly harder by requiring an egg – the added effort raised their appreciation.

#### Steps

#### Step 1: Identify Influential Authority Figures

Try it out: whenever you have invested a lot of time and effort into something, stand back and examine the result – only the result.

#### Best Practices

- ✅ Try it out: whenever you have invested a lot of time and effort into something, stand back and examine the result – only the result.

#### Keep In Mind

You will encounter effort justification whenever you value a self-assembled IKEA shelf more than an identical ready-made one, or when you defend a project simply because you worked hard on it.

#### Security & Safety Notes

- This bias can cause individuals and organizations to continue investing in failing projects to justify previous investments, resulting in inefficient resource allocation.

#### Common Pitfalls

- At last, his superior ofﬁcer stands in front of him, lines the pin up against his chest and pounds it in so hard that it pierces John’s ﬂesh.
- To have a hole punched in your chest for a simple merit badge borders on the absurd.
- All of this happens unconsciously and is difficult to prevent.

### Lesson 61: The Law of Small Numbers

#### Examples

A retail company with 1,000 stores finds both the highest and lowest shoplifting rates in rural branches – until realizing small stores fluctuate wildly while large ones cluster around the average. A mega-branch with 1,000 employees has a stable average weight of 170 pounds, but a mini-branch with just two workers sees its average swing dramatically depending on who is hired or fired. A study claiming 'start-ups hire MENSA material' is meaningless: small businesses naturally produce the highest and lowest IQ averages simply due to chance. Nobel Prize winner Daniel Kahneman notes that even experienced scientists succumb to the law of small numbers.

#### Steps



#### Step 1: Always Factor in Sample Size

Before treating a statistic as meaningful, check the size of the group it comes from. A retail branch with two employees will see its average weight swing dramatically depending on who is hired or fired, while a branch with 1,000 employees has a stable average. The smaller the group, the more its statistics will fluctuate purely by chance.

#### Step 2: Don't Overinterpret Extreme Small-Group Results

When you hear that small businesses have the highest average IQs, or that the shops with the worst shoplifting rates are all in rural areas, ask yourself whether the pattern is a natural consequence of small numbers. Small entities will always appear at both the top and bottom of any ranking — that is how random distribution works.

#### Step 3: Remember That Even Scientists Fall for This

Nobel Prize winner Daniel Kahneman noted that even experienced researchers succumb to the law of small numbers. If a study on small companies or small classrooms produces a surprising result, treat it with scepticism — it may be nothing more than a humdrum consequence of chance.

#### Best Practices

- ✅ Let's go back to the shoplifting problem: a retail company with 1,000 stores finds both the highest and lowest shoplifting rates in rural branches — until realising small stores fluctuate wildly while large ones cluster around the average.
- ✅ From now on, we will install additional safety systems in all rural branches. So, the CEO's conclusion was useless, but at least he doesn't need to go overboard on a security system for the small stores.
- ❌ Nobel Prize winner Daniel Kahneman notes that even experienced scientists succumb to the law of small numbers.

#### Keep In Mind

You will encounter the law of small numbers whenever you draw sweeping conclusions from a handful of observations, such as concluding that a teaching method works based on one successful class.

#### Security & Safety Notes

- From now on, we will install additional safety systems in all rural branches.
- So, the CEO’s conclusion was useless, but at least he doesn’t need to go overboard on a security system for the small stores.

#### Common Pitfalls

- Let’s go back to the shoplifting problem.

---

### Lesson 62: Expectations

#### Examples

##### Example 1: Experiment

In 1965, the American psychologist Robert Rosenthal conducted a noteworthy experiment in schools. Teachers were told of a (fake) new test that identifies students who were on the verge of an intellectual spurt – so-called ‘bloomers’. Twenty per cent of students were randomly selected and classiﬁed as such. Teachers remained under the impression that these were indeed high-potential students. After a year, Rosenthal discovered that these students had developed much higher IQs than other children in a control group. This effect became known as the Rosenthal effect (or Pygmalion effect). Unlike the CEOs and CFOs who consciously tailor their performance to meet expectations, the teachers’ actions were subconscious. Unknowingly, they probably devoted more time to the bloomers, and consequently, the group learned more.

#### Steps



#### Step 1: Raise Expectations for What You Control

Raise expectations for yourself and for the people you love. The Rosenthal experiment showed that teachers who were told certain students were 'bloomers' unconsciously devoted more time to them, and those students' IQs rose statistically significantly. Positive expectations from people you care about increase motivation and can literally change outcomes.

#### Step 2: Lower Expectations for What You Cannot Control

Lower expectations for things outside your control — such as the stock market, the weather, or other people's decisions. When Google's revenue rose 97% but analysts had anticipated even more, the stock plunged 16% in seconds. The best shield against nasty surprises is to anticipate disappointment.

#### Step 3: Understand the Placebo Power of Expectations

Expectations alter the biochemistry of the brain and therefore the whole body. The placebo effect works for roughly one-third of all patients. Recognise that your expectations about health, performance, or subjective well-being are not neutral — they actively shape your experience. Use them deliberately.

#### Best Practices

- ✅ This brings us to the placebo effect — pills and therapies that are unlikely to improve health, but do so anyway.
- ✅ Is it possible to live a life free from expectations? Teachers who were told that 20% of their students were 'bloomers' devoted more time to those students, and their IQs rose accordingly.
- ❌ Rosenthal discovered that the teachers' actions were subconscious: they were not consciously favouring the bloomers, yet their expectations shaped reality.

#### Keep In Mind

You will encounter the expectations bias whenever a teacher's belief that certain students are gifted actually causes those students to perform better, or when a CEO's expectations of employee performance become self-fulfilling prophecies.

#### Security & Safety Notes

- This brings us to the placebo effect – pills and therapies that are unlikely to improve health, but do so anyway.
- Is it possible to live a life free from expectations?

#### Common Pitfalls

- See also Black Swan (ch. 75); Forecast Illusion (ch. 40); Halo Effect (ch. 38)

---

### Lesson 63: Simple Logic

#### Examples

Professor Shane Frederick's Cognitive Reflection Test asks three questions: a paddle and ball costing $1.10 where the paddle costs $1 more, five machines making five shirts in five minutes, and water lilies covering a pond in 48 days. The intuitive answers (10 cents, 100 minutes, 24 days) are all wrong – the correct answers are 5 cents, 5 minutes, and 47 days. MIT students averaged 2.18 correct answers while University of Michigan students scored only 0.83. Low scorers prefer $3,400 now over $3,800 in a month, are more likely to believe in God, and think the average speed of a 100 mph outbound and 50 mph return trip is 75 mph.

#### Steps

#### Step 1: Pause Before Accepting Intuitive Answers

Here’s a hint: would you prefer a bird in the hand or two in the bush?

#### Step 2: Distinguish Intuition from Rational Thinking

Frederick discovered that people with low CRT results tend to prefer a bird in the hand.

#### Step 3: Practice Slow Thinking on Complex Problems

Frederick put the following question to the participants: ‘Would you rather have $3,400 now or $3,800 in a month?’ In general, people with low CRT scores favour getting the smaller amount sooner.

#### Best Practices

- ✅ Here's a hint: would you prefer a bird in the hand or two in the bush?
- ✅ Frederick discovered that people with low CRT results tend to prefer a bird in the hand.
- ❌ Frederick put the following question to the participants: 'Would you rather have $3,400 now or $3,800 in a month?' In general, people with low CRT scores favour getting the smaller amount sooner.

#### Keep In Mind

You will encounter simple logic bias whenever you answer 10 cents to the paddle-and-ball question, or when you prefer $3,400 now over $3,800 in a month despite the math being straightforward.

#### Security & Safety Notes

- Those who score at least 2 or higher usually opt for the riskier option.

#### Common Pitfalls

- Those who score at least 2 or higher usually opt for the riskier option.
- For them, waiting poses a challenge because they are more impulsive.
- See also Hyperbolic Discounting (ch. 51); Decision Fatigue (ch. 53); Exponential Growth (ch. 34); Gambler’s Fallacy (ch. 29); The Problem With Averages (ch. 55)

### Lesson 64: Forer Effect

#### Examples

##### Example 1: Experiment

The experiment was repeated in the decades that followed, with virtually identical results. Most likely you gave the text a 4 or 5, too. People tend to identify their own traits in such universal descriptions. Science labels this tendency the Forer effect (or the Barnum effect). The Forer effect explains why the pseudo-sciences work so well – astrology, astrotherapy, the study of handwriting, biorhythm analysis, palmistry, tarot-card readings and séances with the dead. What’s behind the Forer effect? First, the majority of statements in Forer’s passage are so general that they relate to everyone: ‘Sometimes you seriously doubt your actions.’ Who doesn’t? Second, we tend to accept ﬂattering statements that don’t apply to us: ‘You are proud of your independent thinking.’ Obviously!

#### Steps

#### Step 1: Pause Before Accepting Intuitive Answers

You prefer a certain amount of change and variety and become dissatisﬁed when hemmed in by restrictions and limitations.

#### Step 2: Expose the Barnum Statement

You value independent thinking and require evidence before accepting claims, even when they come from authoritative sources.

#### Step 3: Recognize the Feature-Positive Effect

Second, we tend to accept ﬂattering statements that don’t apply to us: ‘You are proud of your independent thinking.’ Obviously!

#### Step 4: Test Claims Against All Cards

Fourth, the father of all the fallacies, the conﬁrmation bias: we accept whatever corresponds to our self- image and unconsciously ﬁlter everything else out.

#### Best Practices

- ✅ To ensure anonymity, participants never ﬁnd out their numbers.

#### Keep In Mind

You will encounter the Forer effect whenever you read your horoscope and find it eerily accurate, or when a personality test gives you a description that fits everyone.

#### Security & Safety Notes

- Disciplined and self- controlled outside, you tend to be worrisome and insecure inside.
- Security is one of your major goals in life.’ Do you recognise yourself?

#### Common Pitfalls

- Your sexual adjustment has presented problems for you.

---

### Lesson 65: Volunteer’s Folly

#### Examples

Jack, a fashion photographer earning $500 an hour, is asked to volunteer building birdhouses for endangered species next Saturday. Economists call this volunteer's folly: it would be far more effective for Jack to work an extra hour and hire a professional carpenter for six hours with the $400 difference, making better birdhouses than he ever could. More than one-fourth of Americans volunteer their time, but unless Jack is a celebrity whose participation generates publicity, the best way to contribute is with money rather than labour. Strictly speaking, anyone who profits or feels satisfaction from volunteering is not a pure altruist.

#### Steps



#### Step 1: Calculate Your Hourly Value Before Volunteering

If you earn $500 an hour and a carpenter earns $50 an hour, working one extra hour and hiring the carpenter for six hours produces far more value than cobbling together birdhouses yourself for the same period. Before saying yes to hands-on volunteer work, calculate whether your time would be better spent earning money to pay a professional.

#### Step 2: Volunteer Only If Your Expertise Is Irreplaceable

Hands-on volunteer work makes sense only when you can apply a skill no one else can. If the bird club needs a professional photo for a fundraising campaign, you shoot it yourself or work an extra hour to hire another top photographer and donate the difference. Apply your comparative advantage.

#### Step 3: Ask Whether You Generate Publicity

There is one group exempt from volunteer's folly: celebrities. If Bono, Kate Winslet, or Mark Zuckerberg build a birdhouse, their participation is worth more than a professional carpenter's because of the publicity it generates. If people don't double-take when they pass you on the street, your best contribution is financial, not physical labour.

#### Best Practices

- ✅ Jack is well known on the social circuit, and the money is great: $500 an hour, easy. 'That's as much as a commercial lawyer,' he brags to his buddies.
- ✅ Jack leads an enviable life, but lately he has become more philosophical. We're looking for volunteers to help us build birdhouses for endangered species.
- ❌ Unless Jack is a celebrity whose participation generates publicity, the best way to contribute is with money rather than labour.

#### Keep In Mind

You will encounter volunteer's folly whenever you spend a Saturday building birdhouses when you would have worked an extra hour and hired a professional carpenter for six hours.

#### Security & Safety Notes

- Jack is well known on the social circuit, and the money is great: $500 an hour, easy. ‘That’s as much as a commercial lawyer,’ he brags to his buddies, ‘and what I have in front of my lens looks a lot better than any banker.’ Jack leads an enviable life, but lately he has become more philosophical.
- We’re looking for volunteers to help us build birdhouses for endangered species.

#### Common Pitfalls

- We’re looking for volunteers to help us build birdhouses for endangered species.

---

### Lesson 66: Affect Heuristic

#### Examples

##### Example 1: Sunshine and Stock Markets (Affect Heuristic)

They found a correlation that reads much like a farmer’s adage: if the sun is shining in the morning, the stock market will rise during the day. Not always, but often. Who would have thought that sunshine move billions? Studies have found a correlation between sunny mornings and positive stock market performance, similar to the effect of positive emotional cues on decision-making. Whether we like it or not, we are puppets of our emotions. We make complex decisions by consulting our feelings, not our thoughts. Against our best intentions, we substitute the question, ‘What do I think about this?’ with ‘How do I feel about this?’ So, smile! Your future depends on it.

#### Steps

#### Step 1: Stay Skeptical of Predictions

Studies have found a correlation between sunny mornings and positive stock market performance, similar to the effect of positive emotional cues on decision-making.

#### Step 2: Question Your Emotional Reactions

Against our best intentions, we substitute the question, ‘What do I think about this?’ with ‘How do I feel about this?’ So, smile!

#### Best Practices

- ✅ Studies have found a correlation between sunny mornings and positive stock market performance, similar to the effect of positive emotional cues on decision-making.
- ✅ Against our best intentions, we substitute the question, 'What do I think about this?' with 'How do I feel about this?' So, smile!
- ❌ List all the disadvantages, estimate their potential damage and multiply them by the likelihood of them happening.

#### Keep In Mind

You will encounter the affect heuristic whenever sunshine makes you optimistic about the stock market, or when your feelings about a risk override the actual statistics.

#### Security & Safety Notes

- List all the disadvantages, estimate their potential damage and multiply them by the likelihood of them happening.
- This automatic, one- dimensional impulse prevents you from considering risks and beneﬁts to be independent variables, which indeed they are.

#### Common Pitfalls

- It is hard to imagine a storm of the century if you’re only 30 years old.
- In our evolutionary past, whoever thought too long and hard vanished inside a predator’s jaws.
- This automatic, one- dimensional impulse prevents you from considering risks and beneﬁts to be independent variables, which indeed they are.

### Lesson 67: Introspection Illusion

#### Examples

Bruce, president of a vitamin company founded by his father, has taken three multivitamins daily since childhood and is 'sure' they work. Psychologist Petter Johannson showed participants two portrait photos, had them choose the more attractive face, then secretly swapped the photos – most participants then justified in detail why they preferred the image they hadn't actually chosen. When someone disagrees with our firmest beliefs, we instinctively react in one of three ways: assuming they lack information, assuming they're too stupid to understand, or assuming they have evil intentions – political activists, bureaucrats, and religious leaders all fall into these patterns.

#### Steps

#### Step 1: Become Your Own Toughest Critic

Then he showed them the preferred photo up close and asked them to describe the most attractive features.

#### Step 2: Recognize the Three Reactions to Disagreement

Most participants failed to notice and proceeded to justify, in detail, why they favoured the image.

#### Step 3: Beware Introspection Fabrication

In conclusion: nothing is more convincing than your own beliefs.

#### Best Practices

- ✅ Then Petter Johannson showed them the preferred photo up close and asked them to describe the most attractive features.
- ✅ Most participants failed to notice and proceeded to justify, in detail, why they favoured the image.
- ❌ In conclusion: nothing is more convincing than your own beliefs — even when they are based on a photo swap you never noticed.

#### Keep In Mind

You will encounter the introspection illusion whenever you are certain you know why you prefer something, even though your reasons were invented after the fact.

#### Security & Safety Notes

- His father founded the company when supplements were not yet a lifestyle product; a doctor had to prescribe them.
- All his life Bruce has gulped down pills, so he’ll never admit that it was a waste of time.’ For you, however, it’s a different story: you have searched deep inside.

#### Common Pitfalls

- Since childhood, hardly a day has passed without him swallowing at least three multivitamins.
- The belief that reﬂection leads to truth or accuracy is called the introspection illusion.
- Unfortunately, introspection is often unreliable, leading to two main problems: ﬁrst, the introspection illusion creates inaccurate predictions of future mental states.

### Lesson 68: Inability to Close Doors

#### Examples

The author keeps two dozen books beside his bed, dipping into all of them without finishing any. He knows a man dating three women who can't bear to choose one and lose the others. In 206 BC, General Xiang Yu burned his fleet after crossing the Yangtze, telling his troops: 'You now have a choice: fight to win or die.' Cortés sank his own ships upon landing in Mexico. In Dan Ariely and Jiwoong Shin's computer game, players frantically rushed between shrinking doors to keep all options open, scoring 15% fewer points than when they focused on the best room – even when opening doors cost three points and they knew exactly what each room contained.

#### Steps

#### Step 1: Document Your Thinking

Adopt a life strategy similar to a corporate strategy: write down what not to pursue in your life.

#### Best Practices

- ✅ Think hard once and then just consult your list instead of having to make up your mind whenever a new door cracks open.

#### Keep In Mind

You will encounter the inability to close doors whenever you keep two dozen books beside your bed without finishing any, or when you cannot bear to choose one partner and lose the others.

#### Security & Safety Notes

- Players now rushed from door to door to secure access to all potential treasure troves.
- In the ﬁnancial markets, things are clear: a ﬁnancial option on a security always costs something.

#### Common Pitfalls

- However, this can easily destroy success.
- Think hard once and then just consult your list instead of having to make up your mind whenever a new door cracks open.
- See also Sunk Cost Fallacy (ch. 5); Action Bias (ch. 43)

### Lesson 69: Neomania

#### Examples

Fifty years ago people imagined highways in the sky, plastic capsule homes, moon vacations, and jetpacks – yet today we sit in Egyptian chairs, wear Germanic pants, use Roman forks, and eat with Stone Age tools. The 1960s obsession with space travel and the 1970s fixation on plastic houses proved as fleeting as the latest iPhone. Nassim Taleb calls this neomania: valuing novelty itself over actual benefit. Stanley Kubrick's 1968 film 2001 predicted a thousand-person moon colony and PanAm space flights by the millennium – neither materialized. Taleb's rule: whatever has survived X years will probably last another X years.

#### Steps

#### Step 1: Question the New

Old technology has proven itself; it possesses an inherent logic even if we do not always understand it.

#### Best Practices

- ✅ Old technology has proven itself; it possesses an inherent logic even if we do not always understand it.
- ✅ Taleb's rule: whatever has survived X years will probably last another X years.
- ❌ Now I regard them as irrational and suffering from a form of irrationality: neomania — the mania for all things shiny and new.

#### Keep In Mind

You will encounter neomania whenever you discard a reliable tool for a flashy new app, or when a company replaces a working system simply because it is no longer novel.

#### Security & Safety Notes

- What will your everyday life be like?
- Our best friends would be robots, death would be cured and we would have exchanged our bikes for jetpacks long ago.

#### Common Pitfalls

- Taleb traces this tendency back to the neomania pitfall: the mania for all things shiny and new.
- Now I regard them as irrational and suffering from a form of irrationality: neomania.

---

### Lesson 70: Sleeper Effect

#### Examples

During World War II, propaganda films failed to increase soldiers' war enthusiasm when measured immediately – they were dismissed as propaganda. But nine weeks later, soldiers who had seen the films expressed statistically significantly more support for the war than those who hadn't. Psychologist Carl Hovland discovered that the source of an argument fades from memory faster than the argument itself, so untrustworthy information paradoxically gains credibility over time. Political attack ads work the same way: the messenger fades, but the accusations persist. The defence is to actively remember where information came from and ask: who benefits?

#### Steps

#### Step 1: Remember the Source of Every Argument

Immediately after reading it you are a zealous convert, but after weeks, you don’t really remember why.

#### Step 2: Avoid Ad-Contaminated Sources

Even careful, well-informed readers may occasionally fail to remember the source of a claim.

#### Step 3: Remember the Key Point

It’s quite possible that, after weeks, you won’t remember if you picked up certain information from a well-researched article or from a tacky advertorial.

#### Step 4: Time-Test Persuasive Messages

First, don’t accept any unsolicited advice, even if it seems well meant.

#### Best Practices

- ✅ Immediately after reading it you are a zealous convert, but after weeks, you don't really remember why.
- ✅ Even careful, well-informed readers may occasionally fail to remember the source of a claim.
- ❌ First, don't accept any unsolicited advice, even if it seems well meant. It's quite possible that, after weeks, you won't remember if you picked up certain information from a well-researched article or from a tacky advertorial.

#### Keep In Mind

You will encounter the sleeper effect whenever political attack ads make you forget who paid for them, or when a friend's recommendation fades but the advice lingers.

#### Security & Safety Notes

- It has a half-life like a radioactive substance.
- Doing so, you protect yourself to a certain degree from manipulation.

#### Common Pitfalls

- Probe the issue like an investigator would: cui bono?
- See also Framing (ch. 42); Primacy and Recency Effects (ch. 73); News Illusion (ch. 99)

### Lesson 71: Alternative Blindness

#### Examples

An MBA brochure claims graduates earn $400,000 more over their careers, but this ignores four fallacies: the swimmer's body illusion, two years of lost income during study, the impossibility of predicting earnings thirty years out, and most importantly, alternative programs that cost less and deliver similar benefits. A broker recommends a 5% bond over a 1% savings account, but without comparing it to all other investments, the comparison is meaningless – Warren Buffett evaluates every deal against the second-best alternative. A city planning a sports arena on vacant land compares it to an empty lot, not to the school, hospital, or arts centre that same land fund instead.

#### Steps

#### Step 1: Remember the Key Point

The bottom line: if you have trouble making a decision, remember that the choices are broader than ‘no surgery’ or ‘highly risky surgery’.

#### Best Practices

- ✅ The bottom line: if you have trouble making a decision, remember that the choices are broader than 'no surgery' or 'highly risky surgery'.
- ✅ During this time you can expect a loss of earnings — say, $100,000. Let's say your doctor discovers a tumour that will kill you in five years.
- ❌ First, we have the swimmer's body illusion: MBA programmes attract career-oriented people who will probably earn above-average salaries at some stage of their careers, even without the extra qualification of an MBA.

#### Keep In Mind

- The bottom line: if you have trouble making a decision, remember that the choices are broader than ‘no surgery’ or ‘highly risky surgery’.

#### Security & Safety Notes

- During this period, you can expect a substantial loss of earnings — for example, approximately $100,000 over two years.
- Let’s say your doctor discovers a tumour that will kill you in ﬁve years.

#### Common Pitfalls

- First, we have the swimmer’s body illusion: MBA programmes attract career-oriented people who will probably earn above-average salaries at some stage of their careers, even without the extra qualiﬁcation of an MBA.
- The second fallacy: an MBA takes two years.
- However, this procedure is highly risky with a survival rate of just 50%.

### Lesson 72: Social Comparison Bias

#### Examples

As the author's book reached number one, his publisher asked for a testimonial for a rival's book that soon compete for the top slot – and he hesitated, realizing social comparison bias had kicked in. In academia, a Nobel laureate prevented a promising young colleague from applying for a job at his university. Guy Kawasaki observed that A-players hire people better than themselves, B-players hire C-players to feel superior, and C-players hire D-players – creating a 'bozo explosion' where organizations gradually fill with underqualified people. Isaac Barrow gave up his Oxford professorship so Isaac Newton would have it; when was the last time a CEO cleared out for a better employee?

#### Steps

#### Step 1: Welcome More Talented Colleagues

In conclusion: do you foster individuals more talented than you?

#### Best Practices

- ✅ In conclusion: do you foster individuals more talented than you?
- ✅ Book testimonials are a harmless example of the social comparison bias.
- ❌ While hiring people more talented than you may temporarily reduce your relative standing, the overall performance of the organization improves, benefiting everyone including you.

#### Keep In Mind

You will encounter social comparison bias whenever your publisher asks for a testimonial for a rival's book that soon compete for the top slot, and you hesitate.

#### Security & Safety Notes

- Book testimonials are a harmless example of the social comparison bias.
- While hiring people more talented than you may temporarily reduce your relative standing, the overall performance of the organization improves, benefiting everyone including you.

#### Common Pitfalls

- That’s social comparison bias hard at work.
- Garcia suggests that social comparison bias may well be the reason why hardly any research groups remain at the top for years in succession.
- They suffer from illusory superiority, which leads them to make even more thinking errors, thus creating a vicious cycle that erodes the talent pool over time.

### Lesson 73: Primacy and Recency Effects

#### Examples

Most people prefer Alan (smart, hard-working, impulsive, critical, stubborn, jealous) over Ben (jealous, stubborn, critical, impulsive, hard-working, smart) even though the traits are identical – the first impressions dominate. Daniel Kahneman discovered this affected his exam grading until he switched to evaluating all answers to question one before moving to question two. In job interviews, the first candidate sets an anchor that influences all subsequent evaluations, and in meetings, the first opinion shared shapes the group's final decision. The recency effect dominates when impressions formed weeks ago are recalled, and the author scores interviews every five minutes to calculate an objective average.

#### Steps

#### Step 1: Batch Your Evaluations to Avoid Primacy Bias

Without thinking about it too long, decide who you prefer.

#### Step 2: Randomize the Order of Opinions

Who would you prefer to get stuck in an elevator with?

#### Step 3: Account for the Recency Effect

Unfortunately, this trick is not always replicable.

#### Step 4: Avoid First-Impression Evaluations

If, however, you are chairing the committee, always ask members’ opinions in random order so that no one has an unfair advantage.

#### Best Practices

- ✅ This way, I make sure that the ‘middle’ counts just as much as hello and goodbye.

#### Keep In Mind

You will encounter primacy and recency effects whenever you prefer Alan (smart, hard-working, impulsive, critical, stubborn, jealous) over Ben (jealous, stubborn, critical, impulsive, hard-working, smart) even though the traits are identical.

#### Security & Safety Notes

- When recruiting a new employee, for example, you run the risk of hiring the person who makes the best ﬁrst impression.

#### Common Pitfalls

- Alan is smart, hard-working, impulsive, critical, stubborn and jealous.
- Ben, however, is jealous, stubborn, critical, impulsive, hard-working and smart.
- Alan is smart and hard-working.

### Lesson 74: Not-Invented-Here Syndrome

#### Examples

##### Example 1: Real-World Story

I recently had lunch with the CEO of a company that specialises in software for health insurance ﬁrms. He told me how difﬁcult it is to sell his software to potential customers, even though his ﬁrm is the market leader in terms of service, security and functionality. Most insurers are convinced that the best solution is what they have crafted themselves in-house over the past thirty years. Another CEO told me how hard it is to get his staff in the company’s headquarters to accept solutions proposed from far-flung subsidiaries. When people collaborate to solve problems and then evaluate these ideas themselves, NIH syndrome will inevitably exert an inﬂuence. Thus, it makes sense to split teams into two groups. The ﬁrst group generates ideas; the second rates them. Then they swap: the second team comes up with ideas and the ﬁrst team rates them.

#### Steps

#### Step 1: Question Your Own Creations

In conclusion: we are drunk on our own ideas.

#### Best Practices

- ✅ In conclusion: we are drunk on our own ideas.
- ✅ When people collaborate to solve problems and then evaluate these ideas themselves, NIH syndrome will inevitably exert an influence.
- ❌ My cooking skills are quite modest, and my wife knows it. I recently had lunch with the CEO of a company that specialises in software for health insurance firms.

#### Keep In Mind

You will encounter not-invented-here syndrome whenever a team dismisses a brilliant external idea simply because it did not originate from within the group.

#### Security & Safety Notes

- My cooking skills are quite modest, and my wife knows it.
- I recently had lunch with the CEO of a company that specialises in software for health insurance ﬁrms.

#### Common Pitfalls

- Another CEO told me how hard it is to get his staff in the company’s headquarters to accept solutions proposed from far-flung subsidiaries.
- When people collaborate to solve problems and then evaluate these ideas themselves, NIH syndrome will inevitably exert an inﬂuence.
- Writing in his blog at the New York Times , Ariely asked readers to provide solutions to six issues, such as ‘How can cities reduce water consumption without limiting it by law?’ The readers had to make suggestions, and also assess the feasibility of all the ideas proposed.

### Lesson 75: The Black Swan

#### Examples

'All swans are white' was considered watertight for centuries until Willem de Vlamingh spotted a black swan in Australia in 1697. On 19 October 1987, the stock market tumbled 22% with no warning – a Black Swan. Donald Rumsfeld distinguished between known knowns, known unknowns, and unknown unknowns: no one foresaw Facebook mania, making it a Black Swan. Nassim Taleb argues that positive Black Swans favour artists, inventors, and entrepreneurs with scalable products, not employees who sell their time. To survive negative Black Swans: stay out of debt, invest conservatively, and get used to modest living regardless of whether a breakthrough comes.

#### Steps



#### Step 1: Assume Everything Has an Above-Zero Probability

Since probabilities cannot fall below zero and our thought processes are prone to error, treat every scenario as possible. No one foresaw the 1987 stock-market crash, the collapse of the Soviet Union, or Facebook mania. The 'unknown unknowns' are out there, and their impact dwarfs anything we can predict.

#### Step 2: Position Yourself to Benefit from Positive Black Swans

Become an artist, inventor, or entrepreneur with a scalable product. If you sell your time as an employee, dentist, or journalist, you are waiting in vain for a life-changing break. A scalable product — a book, software, or a brand — can deliver returns a thousand times your effort if a positive Black Swan strikes.

#### Step 3: Insulate Yourself from Negative Black Swans

Stay out of debt, invest your savings as conservatively as possible, and get used to a modest standard of living — no matter whether your big breakthrough comes or not. The goal is to survive long enough to be in the game when a positive Black Swan appears, without being wiped out by a negative one.

#### Best Practices

- ✅ But even if you feel compelled to continue as such, avoid surroundings where negative Black Swans thrive.

#### Keep In Mind

You will encounter black swan bias whenever you believe 'all swans are white' until a single black swan in Australia in 1697 — or a 22% stock-market crash on a single October day in 1987 — shatters your certainty.

#### Security & Safety Notes

- A Black Swan is an unthinkable event that massively affects your life, your career, your company, your country.
- The meteorite that ﬂattens you, Sutter’s discovery of gold in California, the collapse of the Soviet Union, the invention of the transistor, the Internet browser, the overthrow of Egyptian dictator Mubarak or another encounter that upturns your life completely – all are Black Swans.

#### Common Pitfalls

- Because, as absurd as it may sound, they are cropping up more and more frequently and they tend to become more consequential.
- Though we can continue to plan for the future, Black Swans often destroy our best-laid plans.
- Back in the Stone Age, we hardly ever encountered anything truly extraordinary.

---

### Lesson 76: Domain Dependence

#### Examples

##### Example 1: Real-World Story

In a previous example, I illustrated base-rate neglect using a medical diagnosis scenario: in a 40-year-old patient, stabbing chest pain may (among other things) indicate heart problems, or it may indicate stress. Stress is much more frequent (with a higher base rate), so it is advisable to test the patient for this ﬁrst. The doctors quickly grasped the concept, demonstrating how expertise in one domain does not always transfer to another. But when I used an example from economics, most faltered. The same thing happens when I speak in front of investors. If I illustrate fallacies using ﬁnancial examples, most catch on immediately. However, if I take instances from biology, some are lost. The conclusion: insights do not pass well from one field to another.

#### Steps



#### Step 1: Test Whether a Principle Actually Works in the New Context

Insights do not pass well from one field to another. Nobel laureate Harry Markowitz devised the optimal portfolio theory but applied only a simple 50/50 split to his own savings — he cannot transfer his academic insight to his personal finances. Before applying a concept from one domain to another, ask: does it actually hold in this new context?

#### Step 2: Expect Knowledge Transfer to Be Harder Than You Think

What you master in one area is difficult to transfer to another, especially from the academic or professional realm to private life. CEOs who are charismatic leaders in the office may be hopeless at home. Doctors who preach health are often heavy smokers. Book smarts does not transfer to street smarts easily.

#### Step 3: Watch for Domain Dependence in Yourself and Others

A software company that recruits a top consumer-goods salesman may find his talents blunted in the new context. A presenter who excels with small groups may tank in front of 100 people. Before promoting someone or entering a new domain yourself, recognise that past success in one field is no guarantee of success in another.

#### Best Practices

- ✅ Insights do not pass well from one field to another: if I illustrate fallacies using medical examples, doctors understand; with financial examples, investors catch on; with biology, some are lost.
- ✅ Before applying a principle from one domain to another, test whether your understanding holds in the new context.
- ❌ In a previous example, I illustrated base-rate neglect using a medical diagnosis scenario: in a 40-year-old patient, stabbing chest pain may indicate heart problems or stress. The doctors quickly grasped the concept, demonstrating how expertise in one domain does not always transfer to another. But when I used an example from economics, most faltered.

#### Keep In Mind

You will encounter domain dependence whenever you understand a fallacy in medicine but fail to recognise it in economics, or when an expert makes elementary errors outside their field of specialty.

#### Security & Safety Notes

- Business leaders and investors invite me to give talks for good money. (Incidentally, this is in itself poor judgement on their part: books are much cheaper.) At a medical conference, the following happened to me.
- In a previous example, I illustrated base-rate neglect using a medical diagnosis scenario: in a 40-year-old patient, stabbing chest pain may (among other things) indicate heart problems, or it may indicate stress.

#### Common Pitfalls

- In a previous example, I illustrated base-rate neglect using a medical diagnosis scenario: in a 40-year-old patient, stabbing chest pain may (among other things) indicate heart problems, or it may indicate stress.
- Markowitz's portfolio selection theory describes the optimum composition of a portfolio, taking into account both risk and return prospects.
- The adrenaline junkie explained to me last week why starting a business is dangerous: bankruptcy can never be ruled out. ‘Personally, I’d rather be bankrupt than dead,’ I replied.

### Lesson 77: False-Consensus Effect

#### Examples

##### Example 1: Real-World Story

A personal example: I was completely convinced that my novel, Massimo Marini, would be a resounding success. It was at least as good as my previous books, I thought, and those had done very well. But the public was of a different opinion and I was proven wrong: false-consensus effect. Of course, the business world is equally prone to such false conclusions. Just because an R&D department is convinced of its product’s appeal doesn’t mean consumers will think the same way. Companies with tech people in charge are especially affected. Inventors fall in love with their products’ sophisticated features and mistakenly believe that these will bowl customers over, too. The false-consensus effect is fascinating for yet another reason.

##### Example 2: Experiment

Ross’s experiment also corroborated this: the students who wore the sandwich board considered those who refused to be stuck up and humourless, whereas the other camp saw the sign-wearers as idiots and attention seekers. You remember the fallacy of social proof, the notion that an idea is better the more people believe in it. Is the false-consensus effect identical? Social proof is an evolutionary survival strategy. Following the crowd has saved our butts more often in the past 100,000 years than striking out on our own. With the false-consensus effect, no outside inﬂuences are involved. Despite this, it still has a social function, which is why evolution didn’t eliminate it. Our brain is not built to recognise the truth; instead its goal is to leave behind as many offspring as possible.

#### Steps

#### Step 1: Assume Your View Is Not the Majority

In conclusion: assume that your worldview is not borne by the public.

#### Best Practices

- ❌ More than that: do not assume that those who think differently are idiots.

#### Keep In Mind

You will encounter the false-consensus effect whenever you are completely convinced that your novel will be a resounding success, only to be proven wrong by the public.

#### Security & Safety Notes

- This bias can lead to costly miscommunication and project failures when people assume their views are mainstream but they are actually niche preferences.

#### Common Pitfalls

- This fallacy is called the false-consensus effect.
- However critical you consider the issue to be, you probably believe that the majority of people share your opinion.
- You remember the fallacy of social proof, the notion that an idea is better the more people believe in it.

---

### Lesson 78: Falsification of History

#### Examples

In George Orwell's 1984, Winston Smith's job at the Ministry of Truth is to revise old newspaper articles so they agree with new developments, creating the illusion of infallibility. Political scientist Gregory Markus asked 3,000 people their opinions on controversial issues in 1973, then re-interviewed them in 1983 – their recalled 1973 responses were almost identical to their 1983 views, not their actual original answers. Ulrich Neisser asked students to write essays about their reactions to the Challenger explosion in 1986; when interviewed three years later, less than 7% of the new data matched the initial submissions. 'I know it's my handwriting, but I couldn't have written this,' one student told him.

#### Steps

#### Step 1: Remember the Key Point

It is safe to assume that half of what you remember is wrong.

#### Best Practices

- ✅ It is safe to assume that half of what you remember is wrong.
- ✅ Revising the past creates the illusion of infallibility and helps governments secure absolute power.
- ❌ In 1973, U.S. political scientist Gregory Markus asked 3,000 people to share their opinions on controversial political issues. In 1986, he asked the same people to recall their 1973 responses — and their recollections were almost identical to their 1983 views, not their actual original answers.

#### Keep In Mind

- It is safe to assume that half of what you remember is wrong.

#### Security & Safety Notes

- Revising the past creates the illusion of infallibility and helps the government secure absolute power.
- Overconfidence in memory can range from trivial errors to life-threatening mistakes, depending on the context.

#### Common Pitfalls

- Revising the past creates the illusion of infallibility and helps the government secure absolute power.
- In 1973, U.S. political scientist Gregory Markus asked 3,000 people to share their opinions on controversial political issues, such as the legalisation of drugs.
- In 1986, the day after the explosion of the Challenger space shuttle, Ulrich Neisser asked students to write essays detailing their reactions.

### Lesson 79: In-Group Out-Group Bias

#### Examples

As a child, the author refused to cheer for Swiss skiers because he didn't know what they thought or read, and if he'd lived nearby over the border he'd have been cheering for another team. British psychologist Henri Tajfel split strangers into groups by tossing a coin, telling one group they shared an art preference – despite being strangers randomly assigned and not knowing anything about art, they found each other more agreeable than outsiders. 'Pseudo-kinship' evokes the same emotions as family without blood relationship, which is why military training forges soldiers as 'brothers' and why people lay down their lives for random groups.

#### Steps

#### Step 1: Question In-Group Loyalty

In conclusion: prejudice and aversion are biological responses to anything foreign.

#### Best Practices

- ✅ In conclusion: prejudice and aversion are biological responses to anything foreign.
- ✅ This distortion is dangerous, especially in business: it leads to the infamous organisational blindness.
- ❌ Such feelings can lead to the most senseless cognitive error of all: laying down your life for a random group — also known as going to war.

#### Keep In Mind

You will encounter in-group out-group bias whenever you refuse to cheer for Swiss skiers because you do not know what they think or read, or when strangers randomly assigned to groups find each other more agreeable than outsiders.

#### Security & Safety Notes

- This distortion is dangerous, especially in business: it leads to the infamous organisational blindness.
- Such feelings can lead to the most senseless cognitive error of all: laying down your life for a random group – also known as going to war.

#### Common Pitfalls

- This distortion is dangerous, especially in business: it leads to the infamous organisational blindness.

### Lesson 80: Ambiguity Aversion

#### Examples

##### Example 1: Anecdote

For this reason, there are similar diseases and it makes sense to say, for example: ‘There is a 30% risk you will die of cancer.’ On the other hand, the following assertion is meaningless: ‘There is a 30% chance that the euro will collapse in the next ﬁve years.’ Why? The economy resides in the realm of uncertainty. There are not billions of comparable currencies from whose history we can derive probabilities. The difference between risk and uncertainty also illustrates the difference between life insurance and credit default swaps. A credit default swap is an insurance policy against speciﬁc defaults, a particular company’s inability to pay. In the first case (life insurance), we are in the calculable domain of risk; in the second (credit default swap), we are dealing with uncertainty. This confusion contributed to the chaos of the ﬁnancial crisis in 2008. If you hear phrases such as ‘the risk of hyperinﬂation is x per cent’ or ‘the risk to our equity position is y’, start worrying.

#### Steps

#### Step 1: Distinguish Risk from Uncertainty

This result is known as the Ellsberg Paradox – named after Daniel Ellsberg, a former Harvard psychologist. (As a side note, he later leaked the top-secret Pentagon Papers to the press, leading to the downfall of President Nixon.) The Ellsberg Paradox offers empirical proof that we favour known probabilities (box A) over unknown ones (box B).

#### Best Practices

- ✅ This result is known as the Ellsberg Paradox — named after Daniel Ellsberg, a former Harvard psychologist.
- ✅ The Ellsberg Paradox offers empirical proof that we favour known probabilities over unknown ones.
- ❌ Thus we come to the topics of risk and uncertainty (or ambiguity) and the difference between them. In the framework developed by Frank Knight, risk refers to situations where probabilities can be calculated from past data, while uncertainty applies when probabilities are unknown.

#### Keep In Mind

You will encounter ambiguity aversion whenever you prefer a known 30% risk of cancer over an unknown 'risk' of economic collapse, or when you choose a bond with a known yield over an investment with an uncertain but potentially higher return.

#### Security & Safety Notes

- Thus we come to the topics of risk and uncertainty (or ambiguity) and the difference between them.
- In the framework developed by Frank Knight, risk refers to situations where probabilities can be calculated from past data, while uncertainty applies when probabilities are unknown.

#### Common Pitfalls

- Thus we come to the topics of risk and uncertainty (or ambiguity) and the difference between them.
- In the framework developed by Frank Knight, risk refers to situations where probabilities can be calculated from past data, while uncertainty applies when probabilities are unknown.
- On the basis of risk, you can decide whether or not to take a gamble.

### Lesson 81: Default Effect

#### Examples

##### Example 1: Experiment

Or consider this experiment: there is a shortage of organ donors. Only about 40% of people opt for it. Scientists Eric Johnson and Dan Goldstein asked people whether, in the event of death, they wanted to actively opt out of organ donation. Making donation the default option increased take-up from 40% to more than 80% of participants, a huge difference between an opt-in and an opt-out default. The default effect is at work even when no standard option is mentioned. In such cases we make our past the default setting, thereby prolonging and sanctifying the status quo. People crave what they know. Given the choice of trying something new or sticking to the tried and tested option, we tend to be highly conservative even if a change would be beneﬁcial.

#### Steps

#### Step 1: Question the Default

The authorities simply need to provide options – always including a default choice for indecisive individuals.

#### Best Practices

- ✅ The authorities simply need to provide options — always including a default choice for indecisive individuals.
- ✅ Making donation the default option increased take-up from 40% to more than 80% of participants.
- ❌ The default option in the first experiment was less expensive but required participants to give up specific compensation rights in case of injury.

#### Keep In Mind

You will encounter the default effect whenever organ donation rates jump from 40% to 80% simply by switching from opt-in to opt-out, or when you stick with the pre-selected insurance plan because evaluating alternatives feels like work.

#### Security & Safety Notes

- The ﬁrst policy was cheaper but waived certain rights to compensation should an accident take place.
- Scientists Eric Johnson and Dan Goldstein asked people whether, in the event of death, they wanted to actively opt out of organ donation.

#### Common Pitfalls

- In my defence, I’m not technically challenged.
- In their book, Nudge, economist Richard Thaler and law professor Cass Sunstein illustrate how a government can direct its citizens without unconstitutionally restricting their freedom.

### Lesson 82: Fear of Regret

#### Examples

##### Example 1: Anecdote

An example: a venerable publishing house stands alone in its refusal to publish trendy e-books. Books are made of paper, asserts the owner, and he will stick by this tradition. Shortly afterward, ten publishers go bankrupt. Nine of them attempted to launch e-book strategies and faltered. The ﬁnal victim is the conventional paper-only publisher. Who will regret the series of decisions most, and who will gain the most sympathy? Right: the stoic e-grumbler. Here is an example from Daniel Kahneman’s book Thinking, Fast and Slow: after every plane crash, we hear the story of one unlucky person who actually wanted to ﬂy a day earlier or later, but for some reason changed his booking at the last minute.

#### Steps

#### Step 1: Act Before the Chance Is Gone

The fear of regret tricked you into thinking this was a one-time offer, when in reality, real estate with a lake view will always come on the market.

#### Best Practices

- ✅ The fear of regret tricked you into thinking this was a one-time offer, when in reality, real estate with a lake view will always come on the market.

#### Keep In Mind

You will encounter fear of regret whenever you panic-buy a property because you fear missing out, or when you hold onto a losing stock to avoid the shame of admitting a mistake.

#### Security & Safety Notes

- Here is an example from Daniel Kahneman’s book Thinking, Fast and Slow: after every plane crash, we hear the story of one unlucky person who actually wanted to ﬂy a day earlier or later, but for some reason changed his booking at the last minute.

#### Common Pitfalls

- The sale of stunning property isn’t going to stop any time soon. ‘Last chances’ make us panic-stricken, and the fear of regret can overwhelm even the most hard-headed dealmakers.

### Lesson 83: Salience Effect

#### Examples

A local journalist named Kurt finds marijuana in a car involved in an accident and writes the headline 'Marijuana Kills Yet Another Motorist' – even though the statistical relationship is zero. When a woman is promoted to CEO, Kurt attributes it to gender despite men holding most top positions, because gender is the salient feature. Nigerian bank robbers reinforce false stereotypes about immigrant criminality. Kahneman and Tversky found that investors react more strongly to sensational news like CEO dismissals than to the less striking but more important information about long-term profit growth.

#### Steps

#### Step 1: Identify the Salient Feature

We always recall the undesirable exceptions – they are particularly salient.

#### Step 2: Fight Seemingly Obvious Explanations

Even professional analysts cannot always evade the salience effect.

#### Step 3: Beware the Itll-Get-Worse Promise

In conclusion: salient information has an undue inﬂuence on how you think and act.

#### Best Practices

- ❌ Do not be blinded by irregularities.

#### Keep In Mind

You will encounter the salience effect whenever a local journalist finds marijuana in a car involved in an accident and writes 'Marijuana Kills Yet Another Motorist' even though the statistical relationship is zero.

#### Security & Safety Notes

- Established newspapers frame drug policy debates with medical data and broader societal considerations, while tabloids focus on sensational individual cases.
- Just as anyone can wind up in an accident, a driver with a joint is also involved in a crash every now and then – purely coincidentally.

#### Common Pitfalls

- Imagine the issue of marijuana has been dominating the media recently.

---

### Lesson 84: House-Money Effect

#### Examples

As a high school student, the author found a 500-Swiss-franc bill (about $250) and immediately bought a top-of-the-range bike with disc brakes and Shimano gears – even though his old bike worked fine and he'd never spend his own mowed-lawn earnings on something so unnecessary. Richard Thaler found that students who'd 'won' $30 took a 70% chance on a $9 coin-toss gamble, while those offered a choice between $30 cash and a different coin toss with equal expected value behaved more conservatively at only 43%. A friend lost $1,000 at roulette and insisted 'I won all that earlier.' Online gambling sites offer $100 signup credit, airlines give frequent flyer miles, and phone companies provide free call credit – all exploiting the house-money effect.

#### Steps

#### Step 1: Treat Wins and Losses Separately

In conclusion: be aware that windfalls and free offers can alter your risk perception and lead to decisions you would not make with your own money.

#### Best Practices

- ✅ In conclusion: be aware that windfalls and free offers can alter your risk perception and lead to decisions you would not make with your own money.
- ✅ Research shows that a substantial portion of lottery winners experience financial difficulties after receiving their winnings, often due to increased risk-taking.
- ❌ That old platitude — win some, lose some — is a feeble attempt to downplay real losses. We treat money that we win, discover or inherit much more frivolously than hard-earned cash.

#### Keep In Mind

You will encounter the house-money effect whenever you gamble with winnings more recklessly than you would with your original stake, or when a free $100 casino credit makes you take risks you would never take with your own money.

#### Security & Safety Notes

- Research shows that a substantial portion of lottery winners experience financial difficulties after receiving their winnings, often due to increased risk-taking.
- That old platitude – win some, lose some – is a feeble attempt to downplay real losses.

#### Common Pitfalls

- However, it never crossed my mind to spend this hard-earned money on something so unnecessary.
- You’ve worked hard for a year.
- We treat money that we win, discover or inherit much more frivolously than hard-earned cash.

### Lesson 85: Procrastination

#### Examples

##### Example 1: The Tax Return That Never Gets Done (Procrastination)

Another friend has tried to quit smoking every day for the past ten years. Each cigarette is his last. My tax returns have been lying on my desk for six months, waiting to be completed. I haven’t yet given up hope that they will ﬁll themselves in. Procrastination is the tendency to delay unpleasant but important acts: the arduous trek to the gym, switching to a cheaper insurance policy, writing thank- you letters. Even New Year’s resolutions won’t help you here. Procrastination is idiotic because no project completes itself. We know that these tasks are beneﬁcial, so why do we keep pushing them on to the back burner?

##### Example 2: Experiment

To bridge it requires a high degree of mental energy, as psychologist Roy Baumeister demonstrated in a clever experiment. He put students in front of an oven in which chocolate cookies were baking. Their delicious scent wafted around the room. He then placed a bowl ﬁlled with radishes by the oven and told the students that they eat as many of these as they wanted, but the cookies were strictly out of bounds. He then left the students alone in the room for thirty minutes. Students in a second group were allowed to eat as many cookies as they wanted. Afterward, both groups had to solve a tough maths problem. The students who were forbidden to eat any cookies gave up on the maths problem twice as fast as those who were allowed to gorge freely on cookies.

#### Steps



#### Step 1: Eliminate Distractions Before Starting

Willpower is like a battery in the short term — it drains with use and needs time to refuel. When writing a novel, turn off your Internet access. Remove every temptation before you begin. If your environment is loaded with distractions, you will exhaust your self-control before you even start the real work.

#### Step 2: Use External Deadlines, Not Self-Imposed Ones

Psychologist Dan Ariely found that deadlines set by external authorities — a teacher, the IRS, a publisher — work far better than self-imposed ones. Self-imposed deadlines work only if the task is broken down step by step, with each part assigned its own due date. Nebulous New Year's resolutions are doomed to fail.

#### Step 3: Make Your Deadlines Public

Transform personal deadlines into public commitments. Tell anyone who will listen about your deadlines, print them on your business cards, or rent a room with no phone or Internet connection. Making your deadlines public is an effective strategy for reducing procrastination, as social accountability increases follow-through. Refuel your willpower battery by eating well, taking breaks, and getting enough sleep.

#### Best Practices

- ✅ Another friend has tried to quit smoking every day for the past ten years. Each cigarette is his last.
- ✅ My tax returns have been lying on my desk for six months, waiting to be completed. I haven't yet given up hope that they will fill themselves in.
- ❌ The period of self-control had drained their mental energy — or willpower — which they now needed to solve the problem. The students who were forbidden to eat any cookies gave up on the maths problem twice as fast as those who were allowed to gorge freely on cookies.

#### Keep In Mind

You will encounter procrastination whenever you delay an unpleasant but important task like filing tax returns, switching insurance, or going to the gym — even though you know the task will only get harder.

#### Security & Safety Notes

- Procrastination can reduce productivity and well-being, leading to stress from rushed work, missed opportunities, and the persistent mental load of incomplete tasks.

#### Common Pitfalls

- Afterward, both groups had to solve a tough maths problem.
- The students who were forbidden to eat any cookies gave up on the maths problem twice as fast as those who were allowed to gorge freely on cookies.
- The period of self-control had drained their mental energy – or willpower – which they now needed to solve the problem.

---

### Lesson 86: Envy

#### Examples

##### Example 1: Hypothetical Scenario

Here is a Russian tale: a farmer ﬁnds a magic lamp. He rubs it, and out of thin air appears a genie, who promises to grant him one wish. The farmer thinks about this for a little while. Finally, he says: ‘My neighbour has a cow and I have none. I hope that his drops dead.’ As absurd as it sounds, you can probably identify with the farmer. Most people experience envy at some point, particularly toward those in their immediate peer group. Imagine your colleague scores a big bonus and you get a gift certiﬁcate. You feel envy.

#### Steps



#### Step 1: Stop Comparing Yourself to the Wrong People

Envy targets those most similar to us in age, career, and residence. You don't envy businesspeople from the last century or millionaires on the other side of the globe — just those on the other side of the city. Aristotle knew this: 'Potters envy potters.' The solution is to stop comparing yourself to people in your immediate peer group and instead measure progress against your own past.

#### Step 2: Build Your Own Castle in a Niche

Find your 'circle of competence' and fill it on your own. Create a niche where you are the best. It doesn't matter how small your area of mastery is — the main thing is that you are king of the castle. As a writer, don't envy musicians or managers; focus on being the best writer you can be. In your own domain, you cannot be rivalled.

#### Step 3: Only Envy the Person You Aspire to Become

When you find yourself suffering pangs of envy, ask: is this envy productive? My wife reminds me: 'It's OK to be envious — but only of the person you aspire to become.' Envy of a neighbour's Porsche is pure waste — the neighbour's gain has cost you nothing. Envy of a version of yourself you are moving toward can be fuel.

#### Best Practices

- ✅ Most people experience envy at some point, particularly toward those in their immediate peer group.
- ✅ Many things spark envy: ownership, status, health, youth, talent, popularity, beauty.
- ❌ When I find myself suffering pangs of envy, my wife reminds me: 'It's OK to be envious — but only of the person you aspire to become.'

#### Keep In Mind

You will encounter envy whenever you feel a pang at your colleague's bonus while you received only a gift certificate, or when a Russian farmer wishes his neighbour's cow dead because he has none.

#### Security & Safety Notes

- Most people experience envy at some point, particularly toward those in their immediate peer group.
- Many things spark envy: ownership, status, health, youth, talent, popularity, beauty.

#### Common Pitfalls

- I hope that his drops dead.’ As absurd as it sounds, you can probably identify with the farmer.
- When I ﬁnd myself suffering pangs of envy, my wife reminds me: ‘It’s OK to be envious – but only of the person you aspire to become.’ See also Social Comparison Bias (ch. 72); Hedonic Treadmill (ch. 46)
